\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi(\phi)}\omega(\phi)+\frac{3}{2\kappa^{2}}\varphi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[-2\frac{\beta h_{s}}{\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\beta+1}}-\varphi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}(\varphi^{\prime}(\phi))^{2}\right]\,.

(134)

Then, for example, when
\beta\neq 1
, if we choose

\displaystyle\varphi(\phi)=\frac{2h_{s}}{1-\beta}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}\,,

(135)

we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi(\phi)}\omega(\phi)+\frac{3}{2\kappa^{2}}\varphi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}=\frac{2{h_{s}}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2\beta}}>0\,.

(136)

Therefore, the condition (
128
) is satisfied.
When
\beta=1
, we may choose,

\displaystyle\varphi(\phi)=2h_{s}\ln\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)\,,

(137)

we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi(\phi)}\omega(\phi)+\frac{3}{2\kappa^{2}}\varphi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}=\frac{2h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2}}>0\,,

(138)

and we find that the condition (
128
) is satisfied, again.

By using (
135
), when
\beta\neq-1
, the action (
IV.2
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}

\displaystyle\,\left[\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2h_{s}}{1-\beta}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}R}{2\kappa^{2}}+\frac{2h_{s}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2h_{s}}{1-\beta}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}}{\kappa^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2\beta}}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{10h_{s}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2h_{s}}{1-\beta}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}}{\kappa^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2\beta}}+L_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(139)

Furthermore, if we define a new scalar field
\xi
by

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{2}{\kappa}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{h_{s}}{1-\beta}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}\,,

(140)

the action (
139
) is further rewritten as

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{\xi^{2}R}{8}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\xi\partial^{\mu}\xi-\frac{5h_{s}^{2}\xi^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1-\beta}{2h_{s}}\ln\frac{\kappa\xi}{2}\right)^{\frac{-2\beta}{1-\beta}}+L_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(141)

On the other hand, when
\beta=1
, by using (
137
) the action (
IV.2
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}

\displaystyle\,\left[\frac{\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2h_{s}}R}{2\kappa^{2}}+\frac{2h_{s}^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2h_{s}-2}}{\kappa^{2}}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{10h_{s}^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2h_{s}-2}}{\kappa^{2}}+L_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(142)

In addition, if we define a new scalar field
\xi
by

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{2}{\kappa}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{h_{s}}\,,

(143)

the action (
139
) is further rewritten as

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{\xi^{2}R}{8}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\xi\partial^{\mu}\xi-\frac{10h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\left(\frac{\kappa\xi}{2}\right)^{2-\frac{2}{h_{s}}}+L_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(144)

We should note that the signature in front of the kinetic term of
\xi
in the action (
141
)
or (
144
) might seem to tell that
\xi
might be a ghost
but as clear in the Einstein frame action (
IV.2
), because the condition (
128
) is satisfied as in
(
136
) or (
138
), there does not appear the ghost in the model.

IV.3
The
k-
essence Model

Here, based on
Matsumoto:2010uv
, we review the reconstruction of
the
k
-essence model, which is a generalization of quintessence theory.
The
k
-essence model is a rather general model that includes only one scalar
field and the action is given by

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{R}{2\kappa^{2}}-K\left(\phi,X\right)+L_{\mathrm{matter}}\right)\,,\quad X\equiv\partial^{\mu}\phi\partial_{\mu}\phi\,.

(145)

Here,
\phi
is a scalar field, again.
The Einstein equation has the following form:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R\right)=-K\left(\phi,X\right)g_{\mu\nu}+2K_{X}\left(\phi,X\right)\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+T_{\mu\nu}\,.

(146)

Here,
K_{X}\left(\phi,X\right)\equiv\frac{\partial K\left(\phi,X\right)}{\partial X}
.
On the other hand, the variation of the action with respect to
\phi
gives

\displaystyle 0=-K_{\phi}\left(\phi,X\right)+2\nabla^{\mu}\left(K\left(\phi,X\right)\partial_{\mu}\phi\right)\,.

(147)

Here,
K_{\phi}\left(\phi,X\right)\equiv\frac{\partial K\left(\phi,X\right)}{\partial\phi}
and it is
assumed that the scalar field
\phi
does not directly couple with the matter.
When we neglect the contribution from the matter, the Friedmann
and Raychaudhuri equations or the FLRW equations are given by

\displaystyle\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}=2X\frac{\partial K\left(\phi,X\right)}{\partial X}-K\left(\phi,X\right)\,,\quad-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)=K\left(\phi,X\right)\,.

(148)

As in the previous model, if we consider the following model

\displaystyle K(\phi,X)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(X+1\right)^{n}K^{(n)}(\phi)\,,\quad K^{(0)}(\phi)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2f^{\prime}(\phi)+3f(\phi)^{2}\right)\,,\quad K^{(1)}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}f^{\prime}(\phi)\,,

(149)

there exists a solution given by (
116
)., again.
Note that in (
149
),
K^{(n)}(\phi)

n\geq 2
can be an arbitrary function but

K^{(2)}
is related with the stability of the solution and
K^{(3)}
is related with the existence
of the Schwarzschild spacetime.
As in the previous models, we can realize the singular behavior in (
107
) by choosing

f(\phi)=\frac{1}{\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\beta}}
.
More explicitly,
K(\phi,X)
in (
149
) is given by

\displaystyle K(\phi,X)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(\frac{2\beta}{\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\beta+1}}+\frac{3}{\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{2\beta}}\right)+\frac{\beta}{\kappa^{2}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\beta+1}}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\left(X+1\right)^{n}K^{(n)}(\phi)\,,

(150)

Then, the model with
\beta\geq 1
generates Type I singularity, the model
0<\beta<1
, Type III,
the model with
-1<\beta<0
Type II,
and the model where
\beta<-1
and
\beta
is not a negative integer generates Type IV singularity.

IV.4
Scalar-Einstein–Gauss–Bonnet Gravity

We now consider the reconstruction of the
scalar-Einstein–Gauss–Bonnet gravity (For pioneering work on the
scalar-Einstein–Gauss–Bonnet gravity, see
Boulware:1986dr
)
based on
Nojiri:2005vv
;
Nojiri:2006je
. The
scalar-Einstein–Gauss–Bonnet gravity was first considered as a
candidate of the DE in
Nojiri:2005vv
.

The action of the Einstein–Gauss–Bonnet gravity with a scalar field
\phi
is

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{R}{2\kappa^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-V(\phi)-\xi(\phi){G}\right]\,.

(151)

Here
{G}
is the Gauss-Bonnet invariant defined by

\displaystyle{G}=R^{2}-4R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}+R_{\mu\nu\xi\sigma}R^{\mu\nu\xi\sigma}\,.

(152)

The variation of the action (
151
) with respect to the scalar field
\phi
yields the following equation:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\phi-V^{\prime}(\phi)+\xi(\phi){G}=0\,.

(153)

When we neglect the contribution from the matter, the variation of the action (
151
) with respect to the metric
g_{\mu\nu}

yields the following field equations,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\left(-R^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}R\right)+\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial^{\mu}\phi\partial^{\nu}\phi-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\phi\partial^{\rho}\phi\right)-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}V(\phi)

\displaystyle+2\left(\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}\xi(\phi)\right)R-2g^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla^{2}\xi(\phi)\right)R-4\left(\nabla_{\rho}\nabla^{\mu}\xi(\phi)\right)R^{\nu\rho}-4\left(\nabla_{\rho}\nabla^{\nu}\xi(\phi)\right)R^{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle+4\left(\nabla^{2}\xi(\phi)\right)R^{\mu\nu}+4g^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{\rho}\nabla_{\sigma}\xi(\phi)\right)R^{\rho\sigma}-4\left(\nabla_{\rho}\nabla_{\sigma}\xi(\phi)\right)R^{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}.

(154)

We should note that the scalar field equation (
153
) can be obtained from Eq. (
IV.4
).
Therefore Eq. (
153
) is not an independent equation and we forget the equation hereafter.

In the flat FLRW spacetime (
3
), Eq. (
IV.4
) has the following forms,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\dot{\phi}}^{2}+V(\phi)+24H^{3}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}\,,

(155)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\dot{\phi}}^{2}-V(\phi)-8H^{2}\frac{d^{2}\xi(\phi(t))}{dt^{2}}-16H\dot{H}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}-16H^{3}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}\,.

(156)

By combining (
155
) and (
156
) and deleting
V(\phi)
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}\dot{H}+{\dot{\phi}}^{2}-8H^{2}\frac{d^{2}\xi(\phi(t))}{dt^{2}}-16H\dot{H}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}+8H^{3}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}\dot{H}+{\dot{\phi}}^{2}-8a\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{H^{2}}{a}\frac{d\xi(\phi(t))}{dt}\right)\,,

(157)

and integrating Eq. (
IV.4
) with respect to
\xi(\phi)
, we get

\displaystyle\xi(\phi(t))=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{8}\int^{t}dt_{1}\frac{a(t_{1})}{H(t_{1})^{2}}\int^{t_{1}}\frac{dt_{2}}{a(t_{2})}\left(\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}\dot{H}(t_{2})+{\dot{\phi}^{2}(t_{2})}\right)\,.

(158)

Finally, by substituting
\xi
in (
158
) into (
155
), we have

\displaystyle V(\phi(t))=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H(t)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\dot{\phi}(t)}^{2}-3a(t)H(t)\int^{t}\frac{dt_{1}}{a(t_{1})}\left(\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}\dot{H}(t_{1})+{\dot{\phi}^{2}(t_{1})}\right)\,.

(159)

Therefore, for the model where
V(\phi)
and
\xi(\phi)
are given by using
adequate functions
g(t)
and
f(\phi)
in the following way,

\displaystyle V(\phi)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}g^{\prime}\left(f(\phi)\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2(f^{\prime}(\phi))^{2}}

\displaystyle\,-3g^{\prime}\left(f(\phi)\right)\mathrm{e}^{g\left(f(\phi)\right)}\int^{\phi}d\phi_{1}f^{\prime}(\phi_{1})\mathrm{e}^{-g\left(f(\phi_{1})\right)}\left(\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}g^{\prime\prime}\left(f(\phi_{1})\right)+\frac{1}{(f^{\prime}(\phi_{1}))^{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\xi(\phi)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{8}\int^{\phi}d\phi_{1}\frac{f^{\prime}(\phi_{1})\mathrm{e}^{g\left(f(\phi_{1})\right)}}{(g^{\prime}(\phi_{1}))^{2}}\int^{\phi_{1}}d\phi_{2}f^{\prime}(\phi_{2})\mathrm{e}^{-g\left(f(\phi_{2})\right)}\left(\frac{2}{\kappa^{2}}g^{\prime\prime}\left(f(\phi_{2})\right)+\frac{1}{(f^{\prime}(\phi_{2}))^{2}}\right)\,,

(160)

the solution of (
IV.4
) is given by

\displaystyle\phi=f^{-1}(t)\quad\left(t=f(\phi)\right)\,,\quad a=a_{s}\mathrm{e}^{g(t)}\ \left(H=g^{\prime}(t)\right)\,.

(161)

Then by choosing

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(t)=\frac{h_{s}}{\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{\beta}}\,,

(162)

we obtain the singular behavior in (
107
).
We should note that as clear from the action (
151
), the ghost does not appear in this model
even if
\beta>0
, that is, the case of Type I and Type III singularities.

It is difficult to execute the integrations in (
IV.4
) for general
\beta
in (
162
).
Then as an example, we consider the following case which corresponds to Type I singularity,

\displaystyle g(t)=-h_{s}\ln\left(t_{s}-t\right)\,,\quad f=t_{s}-f_{s}\mathrm{e}^{\phi}\,.

(163)

Then, we find
g=h_{s}\left(\phi+\ln f_{s}\right)
and

\displaystyle V(\phi)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{f_{s}^{2}\left(h_{s}+1\right)}\left\{\frac{3h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\left(-h_{s}+1\right)-4h_{s}+1\right\}\mathrm{e}^{-2\phi}\,,

\displaystyle\xi(\phi)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{16h_{s}^{2}\left(h_{s}+1\right)}\left(\frac{2h_{s}}{\kappa^{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\phi}\,,

(164)

which is the model proposed in
Elizalde:2004mq
.

IV.5
F(R)
Theories of Gravity

The theory of
F(R)
-gravity is the most simplest and
straightforward generalization of Einstein’s General theory of
Relativity and it has received quite remarkable attention in the
cosmological community for two reasons: One is due to its simple
construction and the other is its ability to explain the late-time
accelerating expansion of the Universe and the early dynamics of
the Universe. Over the last several years, the theory of
F(R)

gravity has been investigated by numerous investigators including
its successes and failures in different domains of astrophysics
and cosmology

Capozziello:2002rd
;
Nojiri:2006gh
;
Capozziello:2006dj
;
Song:2006ej
;
Nojiri:2006be
;
Li:2007xn
;
Sawicki:2007tf
;
Fay:2007uy
;
Boehmer:2007tr
;
deSouza:2007zpn
;
Starobinsky:2007hu
;
Nojiri:2007jr
;
Nojiri:2007as
;
Capozziello:2007ms
;
Nojiri:2007cq
;
Paliathanasis:2011jq
;
Paliathanasis:2016tch
;
Dimakis:2017tvb
;
Papagiannopoulos:2018mez
.
We refer to the review articles on
F(R)
gravity for more
details in this direction

Nojiri:2006ri
;
Sotiriou:2008rp
;
DeFelice:2010aj
;
Nojiri:2017ncd
.

The action of
F(R)
gravity is obtained by replacing the scalar curvature
R
of the Einstein-Hilbert action by a suitable function
F(R)
as follows
Capozziello:2002rd
;
Nojiri:2006ri

\displaystyle S_{F(R)}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{F(R)}{2\kappa^{2}}+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(165)

One can view the modified part in
F(R)
compared to the Einstein-Hilbert part as
F(R)=R+f(R)
.
Now, in the background of a spatially flat FLRW Universe (
3
),
one can derive the gravitational equations in this modified gravitational theory taking the forms:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}\,,\quad p_{\mathrm{eff}}=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}(2\dot{H}+3H^{2})\,,

(166)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}f(R)+3\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)f^{\prime}(R)-18\left(4H^{2}\dot{H}+H\ddot{H}\right)f^{\prime\prime}(R)\right]+\rho\,,

(167)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}f(R)-\left(3H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)f^{\prime}(R)+6\left(8H^{2}\dot{H}+4{\dot{H}}^{2}+6H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)f^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\right]

\displaystyle\,+p\,.

(168)

in which
\rho
,
p
are the energy density and
pressure of the matter sector, respectively, and
R=12H^{2}+6\dot{H}
. If the
matter sector has a constant EoS parameter
w=p/\rho
, then using
Eqs. (
167
) and (
168
), one can quickly
derive that

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}-w\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\cdots\right)\,,

(169)

where

\displaystyle G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right)\,,

(170)

and the explicit form of
G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)
is given by

\displaystyle G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H}\cdots\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\Bigg[\frac{1+w}{2}f(R)-\left\{3\left(1+w\right)H^{2}+\left(1+3w\right)\dot{H}\right\}f^{\prime}(R)

\displaystyle+6\left\{\left(8+12w\right)H^{2}\dot{H}+4{\dot{H}}^{2}+\left(6+3w\right)H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right\}f^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\Bigg].

(171)

The above equations (
170
) and (
IV.5
) have a very important consequence in cosmology.
For example, if a cosmology is given in terms of the Hubble rate
H(t)
, then the r.h.s. of Eq. (
170
)
can be expressed in terms of a function of time, say
f(t)
.
Now, if we can find a combination of
H
,
\dot{H}
,
\ddot{H},...
in
G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)
that reproduces
the function
f(t)
, then the cosmology given by
H(t)
can be realized by this reconstruction mechanism.
Let us illustrate the above by taking an example which will be essential to investigate the singularities in this context.
Assume that a cosmology is given by the following Hubble rate:

\displaystyle H=h_{1}+\frac{h_{2}}{t}\,,

(172)

where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are constants.
Thus, for the choice of
H(t)
in Eq. (
172
), one can derive its time
derivatives as follows:

\displaystyle\dot{H}=-\frac{h_{2}}{t^{2}}\,,\quad\ddot{H}=\frac{2h_{2}}{t^{3}}\,,\quad\cdots\,,

(173)

As a consequence, the r.h.s. of Eq. (
170
)
turns out to be

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(3(1+w)h_{1}^{2}+\frac{6(1+w)h_{1}h_{2}}{t}+\frac{-2h_{2}+3(1+w)h_{2}^{2}}{t^{2}}\right)\,.

(174)

Now, if
G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},..\right)
is given by the following function

\displaystyle G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left\{-3(1+w)h_{1}^{2}+6(1+w)h_{1}H+\left[2-3\left(1+w\right)h_{1}\right]\dot{H}\right\}\,,

(175)

then Eq. (
172
) is a solution of
Eq. (
170
). However, the choice of
G\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\cdots\right)

in Eq. (
175
), is indeed
not unique and there is a large freedom in its choice, but the
choice mainly depends on the underlying gravitational theory where
we are interested in, see for instance Refs.

Capozziello:2006dj
;
Nojiri:2006gh
;
Nojiri:2006be
.

Now, we shall investigate the appearance of finite-time future
singularities in some
F(R)
gravity models using the
reconstruction technique. This phenomenon is not surprising
because the modified gravity can be represented as the Einstein
gravity with an effective ideal fluid having a phantom or
quintessence-like EoS (see the details in Ref.

Capozziello:2005mj
). It is known that in some cases, such
ideal fluid having a phantom or quintessence-like EoS may induce
the finite-time future singularities.

(i) Big Rip singularity:

We start with the Big Rip singularity which is characterized by the following evolution of the Hubble rate
Bamba:2008ut

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{h_{s}}{t_{s}-t}\,,

(176)

where
h_{s}
and
t_{s}
are positive real numbers.
As one can notice, for
t\rightarrow t_{s}
,
H(t)
of
Eq. (
176
) diverges. Now, we shall apply the
reconstruction technique. That means one can reconstruct the
F(R)
gravity theory realizing the cosmology given
in terms of the Hubble rate of Eq. (
176
) which has a Big Rip singularity.

One can rewrite the action of Eq. (
165
) with the use
of proper functions
P(\phi)
and
Q(\phi)
of a scalar field

\phi
as follows
Nojiri:2006gh
:

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\bigg(P(\phi)R+Q(\phi)+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\bigg)\,.

(177)

As the scalar field
\phi
does not have a kinetic term,
therefore, it can be considered to be an auxiliary field.
Now, varying the action (
177
) with respect to
the scalar field
\phi
, one gets

\displaystyle 0=P^{\prime}(\phi)R+Q^{\prime}(\phi)\,,

(178)

where the prime denotes the derivative with respect to
\phi
and this
can in principle be solved with respect to
\phi
as
\phi=\phi(R)
.
Now plugging
\phi=\phi(R)
into the action (
177
), one can certainly express the action in terms of
F(R)
given by

\displaystyle F(R)=P\left(\phi(R)\right)R+Q\left(\phi(R)\right)\,.

(179)

Now by varying the action (
177
) with respect to the
metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
, one can find

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\left\{P(\phi)R+Q(\phi)\right\}-R_{\mu\nu}P(\phi)+\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}P(\phi)-g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}P(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}=0\,,

(180)

where
T_{\mu\nu}
is the energy momentum tensor of the matter
sector. In the background of a spatially flat FLRW Universe (
3
), the
gravitational field equations in Eq. (
180
) reduce to

\displaystyle-6H^{2}P(\phi)-Q(\phi)-6H\frac{dP(\phi(t))}{dt}+\rho=0\,,

(181)

\displaystyle\left(4\dot{H}+6H^{2}\right)P(\phi)+Q(\phi)+2\frac{d^{2}P(\phi(t))}{dt^{2}}+4H\frac{dP(\phi(t))}{dt}+p=0\,.

(182)

By combining Eqs. (
181
) and (
182
) we get

\displaystyle 2\frac{d^{2}P(\phi(t))}{dt^{2}}-2H\frac{dP(\phi(t))}{dt}+4\dot{H}P(\phi)+p+\rho=0\,.

(183)

As one can redefine the scalar field
\phi
, therefore, we may
choose
\phi=t
. Now for the Hubble rate in
Eq. (
176
), one can solve for the scale factor as

\displaystyle a(t)=\widetilde{a}_{0}\exp\left(g(t)\right)\,,

(184)

where
\widetilde{a}_{0}>0
is a constant and
\dot{g}(t)=H(t)
.
Concerning the matter sector we can assume that
\rho=\sum_{i}\rho_{i}
and
p=\sum_{i}p_{i}
where
\rho_{i}
and
p_{i}

denotes the energy density and pressure of the
i
-th fluid, respectively.
If the fluid components do not interact with each other, then using the
usual conservation equation, one can find
\rho_{i}=\rho_{i0}a^{-3(1+w_{i})}
, where
\rho_{i0}

is the current value of the energy density
\rho_{i}
and
w_{i}=p_{i}/\rho_{i}
denotes the EoS
parameter of the
i
-th fluid. With the above considerations, we
can now re-express Eq. (
183
) as

\displaystyle 2\frac{d^{2}P(\phi)}{d\phi^{2}}-2g^{\prime}(\phi)\frac{dP(\phi)}{d\phi}+4g^{\prime\prime}(\phi)P(\phi)+\sum_{i}(1+w_{i})\rho_{i0}a_{0}^{-3(1+w_{i})}\exp\left[-3\left(1+w_{i}\right)g(\phi)\right]=0\,.

(185)

If Eq. (
185
) is solved for
P(\phi)
,
then from Eq. (
181
), one can find
Q(\phi)
as

\displaystyle Q(\phi)=-6\left(g^{\prime}(\phi)\right)^{2}P(\phi)-6g^{\prime}(\phi)\frac{dP(\phi)}{d\phi}+\sum_{i}\rho_{i0}a_{0}^{-3(1+w_{i})}\exp\left[-3\left(1+w_{i}\right)g(\phi)\right]\,.

(186)

Thus, we see that a given expansion history of the Universe
specified by the Hubble rate or the scale factor can be
realized by some specific
F(R)
gravity model.
Now, neglecting the matter sector from this context, the general solution of
Eq. (
185
), can either be given by

\displaystyle P(\phi)=P_{+}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\alpha_{+}}+P_{-}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\alpha_{-}},\quad\alpha_{\pm}\equiv\frac{-h_{s}+1\pm\sqrt{h_{s}^{2}-10h_{s}+1}}{2}\,,

(187)

when
h_{s}>5+2\sqrt{6}
or
h_{s}<5-2\sqrt{6}
or can be
given by

\displaystyle P(\phi)=\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{-(h_{s}+1)/2}\left(\widetilde{A}\cos\left(\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)\ln\frac{-h_{s}^{2}+10h_{s}-1}{2}\right)+\widetilde{B}\sin\left(\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)\ln\frac{-h_{s}^{2}+10h_{s}-1}{2}\right)\right)\,,

(188)

when
5-2\sqrt{6}<h_{s}<5+2\sqrt{6}
. Here
\widetilde{A}
and
\widetilde{B}
are arbitrary
constants.
Now, using Eqs. (
178
), (
179
), and
(
186
) (without matter sector), the forms of
F(R)

for large
R
can be derived as follows:

\displaystyle F(R)\propto

\displaystyle\,R^{1-\frac{\alpha_{-}}{2}}~,\quad\mbox{when}\quad h_{s}>5+2\sqrt{6},\quad\mbox{or}\quad h_{s}<5-2\sqrt{6}\,,

(189)

\displaystyle F(R)\propto

\displaystyle\,R^{\left(h_{s}+1\right)/4}\times\left(\text{oscillating parts}\right),\quad\mbox{when}\quad 5-2\sqrt{6}<h_{s}<5+2\sqrt{6}\,.

(190)

(ii) Other types of singularities

Here we discuss more general singularities appearing in the
context of
F(R)
gravity. In order to proceed with the general
singularities, we consider that the Hubble rate be given in (
61
) or
(
107
) as
Nojiri:2008fk
;
Bamba:2008ut
,
where we assume
h_{s}~(>0)
,
\beta~(\neq 0,1)
are real
numbers,
3
3

3

The case
\beta=0
corresponds to the de Sitter
space and
\beta=1
corresponds to the Big Rip singularity as
discussed above, hence, we are interested to investigate the cases
that
\beta\neq 0,1
.
and
t<t_{s}
since we are living in an
expanding Universe. Note that for non-integer
\beta<0
, some
derivatives of
H
and therefore the curvature may become
singular. For the above Hubble rate in
(
107
), one may realize the evolution of the scale factor as in (
61
).
Notice from Eq. (
61
) that for
non-integer values of
\beta
, when
t_{s}<t
, the scale factor,
and therefore the metric tensor may become complex number which is unphysical.
This could hint towards the ending of our Universe
at
t=t_{s}
even if
\beta
could be negative or less than
-1
.
As we are interested to explore the general singularities, we
focus on
\beta\neq 1
and examine its various ranges. When

\beta>1
, the scalar curvature
R
behaves as

\displaystyle R\sim 12H^{2}\sim 12h_{s}^{2}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-2\beta}\;,

(191)

while for
\beta<1
, the scalar curvature
R
behaves as

\displaystyle R\sim 6\dot{H}\sim 6h_{s}\beta\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\beta-1}\,.

(192)

Now, it is possible to trace the asymptotic solution for
P
when
\phi\to t_{s}
as follows:

1.

For
\beta>1
, one can find the following asymptotic expression of

P(\phi)
:

\displaystyle P(\phi)\sim

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{e}^{\left(h_{s}/2\left(\beta-1\right)\right)\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{-\beta+1}}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\beta/2}\left(\widetilde{A}\cos\left(\omega\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{-\beta+1}\right)+\widetilde{B}\sin\left(\omega\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{-\beta+1}\right)\right)\,,

(193)

\displaystyle\omega\equiv

\displaystyle\,\frac{h_{s}}{2\left(\beta-1\right)}\,.

When
\phi\to t_{s}
,
P(\phi)
tends to vanish.
Using (
178
), (
179
), and (
186
), at large
R
,
F(R)
can be derived as

\displaystyle F(R)\propto\mathrm{e}^{\left(h_{s}/2\left(\beta-1\right)\right)\left(\frac{R}{12h_{s}}\right)^{(\beta-1)/2\beta}}R^{-1/4}\times\left(\mbox{oscillating part}\right)\,.

(194)

2.

For
0<\beta<1
, one gets the asymptotic
expression of
P(\phi)
as follows:

\displaystyle P(\phi)\sim B\mathrm{e}^{-\left(h_{s}/2\left(1-\beta\right)\right)\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{\left(\beta+1\right)/8},

(195)

and
F(R)
is given by

\displaystyle F(R)\sim\mathrm{e}^{-\left(h_{s}/2\left(1-\beta\right)\right)\left(-6\beta h_{s}R\right)^{(\beta-1)/(\beta+1)}}R^{7/8}\,.

(196)

3.

For
\beta<0
, the asymptotic expression of
P(\phi)
is given by:

\displaystyle P(\phi)\sim A\mathrm{e}^{-\left(h_{s}/2\left(1-\beta\right)\right)\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{-\left(\beta^{2}-6\beta+1\right)/8},

(197)

and consequently,
F(R)
is given by

\displaystyle F(R)\sim\left(-6h_{s}\beta R\right)^{\left(\beta^{2}+2\beta+9\right)/8\left(\beta+1\right)}\mathrm{e}^{-\left(h_{s}/2\left(1-\beta\right)\right)\left(-6h_{s}\beta R\right)^{\left(\beta-1\right)/\left(\beta+1\right)}}\;,

(198)

where note that
-6h_{s}\beta R>0
for real solution.

Alternatively, one can trace the behavior of
H
from the behavior
of
R
. Let us consider the case when
R
behaves as,

\displaystyle R\sim 6\dot{H}\sim R_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma}\,.

(199)

Which corresponds to
\gamma=\beta+1<2
. In this situation: If
1<\gamma<2
,
which corresponds to
0<\beta~(=\gamma-1)<1
,
H
is given by

\displaystyle H\sim\frac{R_{s}}{6\left(\gamma-1\right)}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma+1}\,.

(200)

And if
\gamma<1
, which corresponds to
\beta=\gamma-1<0
, then
H
follows

\displaystyle H\sim H_{s}+\frac{R_{s}}{6\left(\gamma-1\right)}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma+1}\,,

(201)

where
H_{s}
is an arbitrary constant
and it does not affect the behavior of
R
. Note that
H_{s}
has
been chosen to vanish in Eq. (
107
).
On the contrary,
if
\gamma>2
, which corresponds to
\beta=\gamma/2>1
, one has
R\sim 12H^{2}
and

H
behaves as

\displaystyle H\sim\sqrt{\frac{R_{s}}{12}}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma/2}.

(202)

Now, for the above expressions of the Hubble rate, one can
find the evolution of the scale factor. If
\gamma>2
, we find
that the scale factor evolves as

\displaystyle a(t)\propto\exp\left(\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}-1\right)\sqrt{\frac{R_{s}}{12}}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma/2+1}\right)\,.

(203)

When
1<\gamma<2
,
a(t)
evolves as

\displaystyle a(t)\propto\exp\left(\frac{R_{s}}{6\gamma\left(\gamma-1\right)}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma}\right)\,.

(204)

And if
\gamma<1
, we get

\displaystyle a(t)\propto\exp\left(H_{s}t+\frac{R_{s}}{6\gamma\left(\gamma-1\right)}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\gamma}\right)\,.

(205)

However, we see that a sudden future singularity appears at

t=t_{s}

Barrow:2004xh
;
Nojiri:2004pf
;
Barrow:2004he
;
Fernandez-Jambrina:2004yjt

when
\gamma<2
. Now, dealing with the case
\gamma<1
, as the
second term in Eq. (
201
) is smaller than the first
term, one may solve the differential
Eq. (
185
) asymptotically in the following
way

\displaystyle P\sim P_{s}\left(1+\frac{R_{s}}{3\beta(1-\beta)}\left(t_{s}-\phi\right)^{1-\beta}\right)\,,

(206)

where
P_{s}
is a constant,
which finally leads to

\displaystyle F(R)\sim F_{0}R+F_{1}R^{2\beta/\left(\beta+1\right)}\,.

(207)

Now, since for
F(R)
gravity, one can introduce the effective
energy density and effective pressure, see Eqs. (
166
), (
167
),
(
168
), thus, for different values of
\beta
of the
Hubble rate (
107
), the nature
of the singularities can be studied as follows. When
\beta>1
,
as
t\to t_{s}
, we see that

\displaystyle a\sim\exp( h_s\left( t_s - t \right)^{1-\beta}/\left( \beta-1 \right) )\to\infty,\;\;\mbox{and as a consequence},\;\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\infty,\,|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to\infty,

(208)

which means that a Big Rip (Type I) singularity occurs. If

0<\beta<1
,
a
goes to a constant but
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\infty,\,|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to\infty
, that means a Type III
singularity occurs. If
-1<\beta<0
, then
a
and
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}

vanish but
|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to\infty
which means that a Type II
singularity occurs. When
\beta<0
, instead of
Eq. (
107
), as in
Eq. (
201
), one may assume, as in (
108
) with
H_{s}(t)
is a constant,

\displaystyle H\sim H_{s}+h_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\beta}\,.

(209)

Now, if
-1<\beta<0
, for
t\to t_{s}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
approaches to finite value
3H_{s}^{2}/\kappa^{2}

but
|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|
diverges, so we have a Sudden singularity.
If
\beta<-1
but
\beta
is not an integer, then
a
remains
finite and
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
vanish if
H_{s}=0
or

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are finite if
H_{s}\neq 0
,
however, higher derivatives of
H
diverge. That means in this
case a Type IV singularity occurs. Thus, we see that
F(R)

gravity may allow various type of finite-time future
singularities. This is not unnatural because in the context of
modified gravity, one can find an effective phantom/quintessence
phase
Nojiri:2006ri
and it is well-known that a
phantom/quintessence-dominated Universe may end up with
finite-time future singularities of various types. Hence, with the
reconstruction of the
F(R)
gravity from a given cosmology one
can find the possible functional forms of
F(R)
that may lead to
finite-time future singularities. For example, from the present
discussions, one can see that
F(R)=R+\widetilde{\alpha}R^{n}

with
n>2
leads to a Type I singularity and
F(R)=R-\widetilde{\beta}R^{-n}
with
n>0
leads to a Type III
singularity where
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
are
any real numbers.

IV.5.1
Occurrence of Singularities in Different Frames:
F(R)
Gravity

The choice of the physical frame in the context of
F(R)
gravity
is an important point because cosmology of an
F(R)
gravity in
one frame could be different in other frame. The accelerating
phase of the Universe in one frame may correspond to its
decelerating phase
Bahamonde:2017kbs
. Also, the type of a
singularity changes from one frame to other frame

Briscese:2006xu
;
Bahamonde:2016wmz
. In this section, we shall
discuss the second possibility in detail, that means, how the
choice of the frames affects the type of finite-time future
singularities appearing in
F(R)
gravity theory.
We start with the vacuum
F(R)
gravity in the Jordan frame whose action is
given by (
165
) when we neglect the contribution from matter by putting
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}=0

Bahamonde:2016wmz
.
Varying the action (
165
) with
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}=0
with
respect to the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
representing a spatially flat
FLRW geometry (
3
), one can obtain the following gravitational field
equations, which is equivalent to (
166
) with (
167
) and (
168
)
when we neglect the matter,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{F(R)}{2}+3\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)F^{\prime}(R)-18\left(4H^{2}\dot{H}+H\ddot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(R)\,,

(210)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\frac{F(R)}{2}-\left(\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)F^{\prime}(R)+6\left(8H^{2}\dot{H}+4{\dot{H}}^{2}+6H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\,,

(211)

where an overhead dot represents the differentiation with respect
to the cosmic time
t
in (
3
) and the prime corresponds to the
differentiation with respect to the Ricci scalar
R
.

Now introducing the auxiliary fields
A
and
B
, the action of
Eq. (
165
) with
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}=0
written in the Jordan frame can
be expressed into an equivalent action as follows
Maeda:1988ab
(see also
Chiba:2003ir
)

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\int d^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[B(R-A)+F(A)\Bigg]\,.

(212)

The action (
212
) can be varied with respect to the auxiliary scalar
B
leading to the condition
A=R
,
and hence, one can recover the action (
165
) with
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}=0
.
Moreover, varying the action (
212
) with respect to
A
, one can eliminate the auxiliary field
B
from it ending up
B=F^{\prime}(A)
.
Hence, the action (
212
) takes the equivalent form

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\int d^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[F^{\prime}(A)(R-A)+F(A)\Bigg]\,.

(213)

Taking a conformal transformation of the metric tensor, one may
obtain a minimally coupled scalar-tensor theory, called the
Einstein frame scalar-tensor theory. We use a particular conformal
factor taking the following expression
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{F^{\prime}(A)}g_{\mu\nu}\,,

(214)

which modifies the Ricci scalar as
R\rightarrow\hat{R}
. With
the conformal transformation (
214
), and
then by defining a new scalar field
\sigma
in terms of the
auxiliary scalar field
A
,

\displaystyle\sigma=-\ln F^{\prime}(A)\,,

(215)

the action (
213
) takes the form

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-\hat{g}}\left\{\hat{R}-\frac{3}{2}\hat{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\sigma\partial_{\nu}\sigma-V(\sigma)\right\}\,,

(216)

where the potential
V(\sigma)
takes the form

\displaystyle V(\sigma)=\frac{A}{F^{\prime}(A)}-\frac{F(A)}{F^{\prime}(A)^{2}}\,.

(217)

Notice that with the use of Eq. (
215
), the
potential of Eq. (
217
) can be expressed in
terms of the scalar field
\sigma
. Thus, corresponding to the
Jordan frame
F(R)
gravity characterized by the action of
Eq. (
165
) with
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}=0
, the Einstein frame scalar-tensor
theory can be found as given by the action of
Eq. (
216
). Finally, we note that with the
use of the following transformation

\displaystyle\varphi=\sqrt{\frac{3}{2\kappa^{2}}}\sigma\,,

(218)

the action (
216
) can be expressed into the canonical form
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left\{\frac{R}{2\kappa^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\mu}\varphi-V(\varphi)\right\}\,,

(219)

Alternatively, starting with the scalar-tensor canonical scalar field action given by
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-\hat{g}}\left\{\frac{\hat{R}}{2\kappa^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\partial}_{\mu}\varphi\hat{\partial}^{\mu}\varphi-V(\varphi)\right\}\,,

(220)

one can find its equivalent Jordan frame
F(R)
gravity action.
Assuming a spatially flat FLRW line element (
3
), the FLRW equations corresponding to
the action (
220
) can be written as

\displaystyle 3\widetilde{H}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}+V\,,\quad 3\widetilde{H}^{2}+2\dot{\widetilde{H}}=-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}+V\,.

(221)

where we have used ‘
tilde
’ in the physical quantities in
this frame (i.e., the Einstein frame) in order to differ the
corresponding physical quantities in the Jordan frame.

Now one can map the above action (
220
) to
a modified
F(R)
gravity theory. In order to do this, we need to
perform the conformal transformation given by

g_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow\mathrm{e}^{\pm\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\kappa\varphi}\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}
, and as a
result, the FLRW metric becomes,

\displaystyle ds_{F(R)}^{2}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\kappa\varphi}\left(-d\widetilde{t}^{2}+\widetilde{a}^{2}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\sum_{i=1,2,3}\left(dx^{i}\right)^{2}\right),

(222)

where we have introduced a new time coordinate
\widetilde{t}
, given by

dt=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\kappa\varphi}d\widetilde{t}
,
the solution of which, denoted by
t=f\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)
, is an
increasing function. Let us note that if the function

f\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)
allows singularities, then we may have some
problems. As we are considering two different frames, i.e., the
Jordan frame and the Einstein frame, therefore, following the
relation between
t
and
\widetilde{t}
, the range of the values
of
\widetilde{t}
could be mapped to a different region in the

t
coordinate. Let us consider an interval

[\widetilde{t}_{1},\widetilde{t}_{2}]
in the Einstein frame, with
the scale factor at
\widetilde{t}=\widetilde{t}_{1}
being equal to

\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{t}_{1}~\right)=0
and at

\widetilde{t}=\widetilde{t}_{2}
, the scale factor is equal to,

\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{t}_{2}~\right)=0
or

\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{t}_{2}~\right)=\infty
. As one can identify
that
\widetilde{t}=\widetilde{t}_{2}
, corresponds to a Big Crunch
or to a Big Rip singularity, respectively, in which case with
potentially
\widetilde{t}_{1}=-\infty
and/or

\widetilde{t}_{2}=\infty
, so that these singularities effectively
do not occur, a fact which crucially depends on the particular
form of the scale factor at hand. The new range of
t
coordinate
will be
[f\left(\widetilde{t}_{1}\right)\,,f\left(\widetilde{t}_{2}\right)]
, assuming

\phi\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)
is regular everywhere in the interval

[\widetilde{t}_{1},\widetilde{t}_{2}]
.

In the following we shall discuss the correspondence of the
finite-time singularities in the Jordan and Einstein frames using
some simple examples. We note that while discussing the
singularities below, we have followed the same unit system as in
Ref.
Bahamonde:2016wmz
, that means, we have considered the
same unit system so that the gravitational coupling constant
becomes unity, i.e.,
\kappa=1
.

(i) Power law cosmology

We consider the power law cosmology which is described by the following scale factor,

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(\widetilde{t}\right)=\widetilde{a}_{c}\left(\frac{\widetilde{t}}{\widetilde{t}_{c}}\right)^{p}\,,

(223)

where
\widetilde{t}_{c}
is a fiducial value of the cosmic time,

p
is a positive real number and from the above relation we
identify that
\widetilde{a}(~\widetilde{t}_{c}~)=\widetilde{a}_{c}
.
Note that such a power law cosmology described in
Eq. (
223
) is a solution of
the Friedmann equation in the Einstein frame scalar-tensor theory
when the potential has the exponential form.
In this case the scalar field evolves as

\displaystyle\phi=\pm\sqrt{2p}\;\ln\left(\frac{\widetilde{t}}{\widetilde{t}_{c}}\right)\,.

(224)

In this case
\widetilde{t}_{1}=0
and
\widetilde{t}_{2}=\infty
and in this model the Hubble rate
\widetilde{H}
diverges at
\widetilde{t}=0
, so we have a
Big Bang singularity in the Einstein frame.

Now in order to understand the behavior of the singularities in
the Jordan frame, we can follow the procedure as described in
Section
IV.5.1
. The new time
variable
t
in the Jordan frame can be found from the differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{d\widetilde{t}}=\left(\frac{\widetilde{t}}{\widetilde{t}_{c}}\right)^{\pm\sqrt{\frac{p}{3}}}\,,

(225)

having the following solution,

\displaystyle t=\frac{3}{3\pm\sqrt{3p}}\widetilde{t}\left(\frac{\widetilde{t}}{\widetilde{t}_{c}}\right)^{\pm 2\sqrt{\frac{p}{3}}}\,.

(226)

The corresponding scale factor as a function of the cosmic time
t
takes the form

\displaystyle a(t)\sim t^{w}\quad\mbox{where}\quad w=\frac{\sqrt{3p}\pm 3p}{\sqrt{3p}\pm 3}\,.

(227)

Now we have the following observations. When the minus sign is
chosen in the conformal factor, the cosmological
evolution (
227
) has a Big
Bang singularity at
t=0
if the power law parameter
p
lies in
the interval
1/3\leq p<3/4
. If
p=1/3
, the Jordan frame metric
becomes static and we do not have any finite-time singularity.
When
0<p<1/3
, the Big Bang singularity at
\widetilde{t}=0
in
the Einstein frame becomes the beginning of a contracting Universe
in the Jordan frame. In the case
3/4<p<3
, from (
226
) we
can see the direction of time is reversed, meaning that this case
must be disregarded. Finally, for
p>3
the time
\widetilde{t}=0

correspond to
t=-\infty
, so the Big Bang singularity disappears
in the Jordan frame.

(ii) Cosmology generated by
R^{-n}
gravity in the Jordan Frame

We now consider the cosmology driven by
R^{-n}
gravity in the
Jordan frame (see
Briscese:2006xu
for details). So, for

F(R)\sim R^{-n}
, from Eqs. (
210
)
and (
211
) one can see that the
corresponding scale factor takes the power law form

\displaystyle a\sim\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{\frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{n+2}}\,.

(228)

Therefore, if either
n<-2
or
-1<n<-1/2
, a Big Rip Type I singularity appears at
t=t_{s}
in the Jordan frame,
and in the remaining cases, a Type III Big Crunch singularity is present at this point.
In this case, the corresponding Einstein frame canonical scalar field takes the form

\displaystyle\sigma\sim(n+1)\ln R\sim-2(n+1)\ln(t_s - t)\,,

(229)

and the Ricci scalar takes the following expression

\displaystyle R\sim\frac{6(n+1)(2n+1)(4n+5)n}{(n+2)^{2}(t_{s}-t)^{2}}\,.

(230)

Now the time coordinate
\widetilde{t}
in the corresponding Einstein frame scalar-tensor theory is given by

\displaystyle d\widetilde{t}=\pm\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}dt\;\;\sim\pm(t_{s}-t)^{-(n+1)}dt\,,

(231)

which gives
\widetilde{t}=\pm(t_{s}-t)^{-n}
. Therefore, for
n>0
,
t
approaches
t\rightarrow t_{s}
in the Jordan frame
corresponds to
\widetilde{t}\rightarrow\pm\infty
in the
Einstein frame. As a consequence, we observe that the singularity
changes its structure abruptly, that means it does not appear in
finite-time in the Einstein frame scalar-tensor theory.
Nevertheless, a new additional singularity may be present, as when

t
approaches infinity in the Einstein frame, it corresponds to
the new time coordinate
\widetilde{t}\to 0
, and thus any
singularities at infinity can be brought back to a finite-time. On
the other hand, when
n<0
, the limit
t\to t_{s}
in the Jordan
frame corresponds to
\widetilde{t}\to 0
in the Einstein frame.
Finally, we note that the metric in the Einstein frame
scalar-tensor theory behaves as

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\mathrm{e}^{\sigma}\left(-dt^{2}+a^{2}(t)\sum_{i=1,2,3}(dx^{i})^{2}\right)\sim-d\widetilde{t}^{2}+\widetilde{a}^{2}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\sum_{i=1,2,3}(dx^{i})^{2}\,,\quad\widetilde{a}^{2}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\sim a_{s}^{2}\;\widetilde{t}^{~\frac{2n(n^{2}-1)}{n+2}}\,,

(232)

where the constant
a_{s}
is a real number. In this case, the power of the scale factor is negative only when
-2<n<-1
or
0<n<1
, and thus a Big Rip singularity is present. Thus, for the Big Rip singularity in the Jordan frame, the scale factor in the Einstein frame behaves as
\widetilde{a}^{2}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\to 0
when
\widetilde{t}\to 0
, thus, it becomes a Type III Big Crunch singularity in the Einstein frame.

(iii) A singular cosmological evolution

In this section, we examine how the simplest singular cosmology behaves in different frames.
We consider the following Hubble rate which describes the simplest singular cosmology
as in (
107
),

\displaystyle H\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)=h_{s}\left(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t_{s}}~\right)^{-\beta}\,,

(233)

where
h_{s}
is any positive real number and
\beta
is a real number. From the values of
\beta
one can determine the singularity type. In particular, we realize the following type of singularities based on the values of
\alpha
:

•

For
\beta>1
, we realize a Type I singularity.

•

For
0<\beta<1
, a Type III singularity is found.

•

When
-1<\beta<0
, a Type II singularity occurs.

•

When
\beta<-1
, a Type IV singularity is realized.

Let us assume that the Hubble rate of
Eq. (
233
) is given in the
Einstein frame and we aim to investigate the singular behavior
captured in the Hubble rate of
Eq. (
233
) in the context of Jordan
frame
F(R)
theory. We use the conformal factor given by

\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}
which results in the
transformation of the metric as

\displaystyle ds_{F(R)}^{2}=\mathrm{e}^{\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\left(-d\widetilde{t}^{2}+\widetilde{a}^{2}(~\widetilde{t}~)\sum_{i=1,2,3}\left(dx^{i}\right)^{2}\right)\,,

(234)

and the scale factor now becomes,

\displaystyle a(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\;\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\,,

(235)

where the Jordan frame time parameter
t
is defined as

\displaystyle dt=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}d\widetilde{t},

(236)

the solution of which is an incomplete gamma function. However, for the Hubble rate as given in
Eq. (
233
), using the corresponding equations of motion, the Einstein frame scalar field can be found to be

\displaystyle\varphi=\frac{2\sqrt{2h_{s}\beta}\left(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~\right)^{\frac{1-\beta}{2}}}{1-\beta}\,,

(237)

where
h_{s}\beta>0
is an essential criterion in order for the scalar field to be canonical.
We further notice that the transformation blows up at
\widetilde{t}_{s}
if
\beta>1
, hence, extra caution is required in this case.
in effect, the new Hubble rate in terms of our initial time coordinate
\widetilde{t}
, i.e.,

H(\tilde{t})\equiv\frac{1}{a}\frac{da}{d\tilde{t}}
, is equal to,

\displaystyle H(\widetilde{t})=\frac{\sqrt{h_{s}\beta}\;(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~)^{-\frac{\beta+1}{2}}}{\sqrt{3}}+h_{s}(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~)^{-\beta}\,,

(238)

and the question is what is the effect on the time coordinate
t
.
We suppose
t=f(~\widetilde{t}~)
. Now, when,
f(~\widetilde{t}~)\neq 0
, we have,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(~\widetilde{t}~)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\,,

(239)

and consequently the following relation

\displaystyle\frac{dH}{dt}=\frac{d\widetilde{t}}{dt}\frac{d\widetilde{H}}{d\widetilde{t}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\frac{d\widetilde{H}}{d\widetilde{t}}\,.

(240)

holds, that means, the expression
dH/dt
diverges if and only if

d\widetilde{H}/d\widetilde{t}
diverges for
\beta<1
, as the
conformal factor is finite at
\widetilde{t}_{s}
. Now the Big Rip
(Type I) singularity occurs when the Hubble rate
H(t)
diverges
at a finite-time thus, we need to examine whether

f(~\widetilde{t}_{s}~)
is finite or not. One can easily derive

t_{s}
given by
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle t_{s}=f(~\widetilde{t}_{s}~)=c_{1}-c_{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{\alpha+1}\right)\,,

(241)

which is finite, provided
2/(1-\beta)
is not a negative integer.
This readily implies that the singularity appears in the Hubble
rate
H
at a finite-time, as long as
\beta\neq-(2/n)+1
where

n\geq 2
is an integer. Accordingly, a Type II singularity is
found if
dH/dt
diverges, but
H
does not diverge. Combining
together, these imply that
-3<\beta<-1
. On the other hand, for

\beta<-3
, a Type IV singularity occurs. Now we investigate the
evolution of the scale factor when it is conformally transformed.
The evolution of the scale factor in the Einstein frame reads, as in (
61
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)=\widetilde{a}_{s}\exp\left(\frac{h_{s}\left(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~\right)^{1-\beta}}{1-\beta}\right)\,,

(242)

which in the Jordan frame reads,

\displaystyle a\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)=a_{s}\exp\left(\frac{3h_{s}\left(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~\right)^{1-\beta}\pm 2\sqrt{3\beta h_{s}}\left(~\widetilde{t}-\widetilde{t}_{s}~\right)^{\frac{1-\beta}{2}}}{3(1-\beta)}\right)\,.

(243)

Now, depending on the values of
\beta
, from the scale factor (
243
),
one can witness the following type of singularities:

1.

For
\beta>1
, a Type I or no singularity occurs.

2.

For
-1<\beta<1
, a Type III singularity occurs.

3.

For
-3<\beta<-1
, a Type II singularity occurs.

4.

For
\beta<-3
, a Type IV singularity occurs.

In TABLE
1
, we show the correspondence
of the finite-time singularities between the Jordan frame and the
Einstein frame. TABLE
1
clearly
shows that the singularity in one frame may not be same in the
other frame. For example, as displayed in TABLE
1
, the Type I (Big Rip) singularity in
the Einstein frame may correspond to a non-singular evolution in
the Jordan frame. Further, the Type II singularity in the Einstein
frame could be modified to a more severe Type III singularity in
the Jordan frame, and the Type IV singularity in the Einstein
frame may correspond to a Type II singularity in the Jordan frame.

Table 1:
The table shows the correspondence of finite-time
singularities in the Einstein and Jordan frames for the
cosmological evolution given in terms of the Hubble rate
(
233
) in the Einstein frame.

Singularity in the Einstein Frame

Singularity in the Jordan Frame

Type I

Type I or No Singularity

Type II

Type III

Type III

Type III

Type IV

Type IV or Type II

We close this section with a
special case of the singular evolution (
233
) describing the singular bounce cosmology,
a special case of the symmetric bounce
Nojiri:2015sfd
;
Nojiri:2016ppu
;
Nojiri:2016ygo
.
One can rewrite the scale factor of the cosmological evolution (
233
) as follows,

\displaystyle a(t)=a_{s}\;\exp\left(h_{s}(t-t_{s})^{2(1+\epsilon)}\right)\,,

(244)

for which the Hubble rate becomes

\displaystyle H(t)=2(1+\epsilon)h_{s}(t-t_{s})^{2\epsilon+1},

(245)

where
\epsilon>0
and it has been chosen in such a way so that all the quantities remain real. Now, in order to realize a bouncing scenario, for
t<t_{s}
the Hubble rate must become negative (i.e.,
H<0
) and additionally, in order for the bounce (
244
) to be a deformation of the symmetric bounce described by
a(t)\sim\exp(\beta t^2)
, the parameter
\epsilon
must lie in the interval
0<\epsilon<1
having the following form

\displaystyle\epsilon=\frac{2n}{2m+1}\,,

(246)

where
m
and
n
are integers and they are chosen in such a way so that
\epsilon<1
is satisfied. Now, for this choice of
\epsilon
, the cosmology described by the scale factor (
244
) and the Hubble rate (
245
) clearly depicts a Type IV singular cosmology, in which case, the Hubble rate and its first derivative with respect to the cosmic time, i.e.,
\dot{H}
remain finite, but its second derivative with respect to the cosmic time, i.e.,
\ddot{H}
diverges. As demonstrated in Refs.
Odintsov:2015jca
;
Odintsov:2015gba
with the use of reconstruction techniques, the pure
F(R)
gravity realizing the cosmological evolution (
245
) can approximately be given by

\displaystyle F(R)=R+\frac{R^{2}}{4C}+\Lambda\,,

(247)

where
C
is a positive real number, near the bouncing point, which is
t\simeq t_{s}
. Let us define a new parameter
x=t-t_{s}
for simplicity. Thus, the limit near the bouncing point, i.e.,
t\simeq t_{s}
, corresponds to the limit
x\rightarrow 0
. Now, in order to transform the theory in the Einstein frame, we consider the following conformal transformation

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{e}^{-\sigma}\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(248)

where the scalar field
\sigma
is equal to
\sigma=-\ln F^{\prime}(A)
.
In terms of the new parameter
x
, the Ricci scalar reads

\displaystyle R=12h_{s}(\epsilon+1)x^{2\epsilon}\Bigg[4h_{s}(\epsilon+1)x^{2\epsilon+2}+2\epsilon+1\Bigg],

(249)

and if we are close to the singularity where
x
is small, then the Ricci scalar becomes,

\displaystyle R\approx 12h_{s}(\epsilon+1)(2\epsilon+1)x^{2\epsilon}\,.

(250)

Consequently, by combining Eqs. (
247
) and (
250
), one obtains

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(R)\approx 1+\frac{6h_{s}(\epsilon+1)(2\epsilon+1)x^{2\epsilon}}{C}\equiv 1+Dx^{2\epsilon}\,,

(251)

where
D
is a positive real number.
This means that the new time coordinate of the Einstein frame FLRW metric will be given in terms of
x
as follows

\displaystyle d\widetilde{t}=(1+Dx^{2\epsilon})^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}}dx\,,

(252)

the solution of which is a hypergeometric function. The new scale factor in terms of
x
reads

\displaystyle a(~\widetilde{t}~)=(1+Dx^{2\epsilon})^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}}\;\widetilde{a}(x)\,,

(253)

and subsequently, the derivative of the scale factor is given by

\displaystyle\frac{da}{d\widetilde{t}}=\frac{dx}{d\widetilde{t}}\frac{da}{dx}=\frac{dx}{d\widetilde{t}}\Bigg[(1+Dx^{2\epsilon})^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}}\frac{d\widetilde{a}}{dx}+2\epsilon x^{2\epsilon}(1+Dx^{2\epsilon})^{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}-1}\widetilde{a}(x)\Bigg]\,.

(254)

As
dt=dx
at
x\simeq 0
, thus, looking at the second derivative of the scale factor
a(t)
, one may conclude that the scale factor diverges at
x=0
provided
\epsilon<1/2
. Hence, the Type IV singularity in the Jordan frame becomes a Type II singularity in the Einstein frame, also reflected from TABLE
1
.

IV.5.2
Occurrence of Singularities in Different Frames: Unimodular
F(R)
Gravity

In this section, we discuss the correspondence between the frames
in the context of unimodular
F(R)
gravity

Nojiri:2015sfd
;
Nojiri:2016ppu
;
Nojiri:2016ygo
;
Odintsov:2016imq
.
The Jordan frame unimodular
F(R)
gravity is described by the
action
Nojiri:2015sfd

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\bigg[\sqrt{-g}\;\bigg(F(R)-\lambda\bigg)+\lambda\bigg]+S_{\mathrm{matter}}\,,

(255)

where we assume that
F(R)
is a smooth function of the Ricci scalar
R
,

\lambda
denotes the Lagrange multiplier function and

S_{\mathrm{matter}}
stands for the action of the matter fluids
present in the Universe sector. Notice that the variation of the
action (
255
) with respect
to
\lambda
leads to the unimodular constraint

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g}

\displaystyle=1\,.

(256)

The unimodular constraint given in
Eq. (
256
) is the key point of
unimodular
F(R)
gravity. Now if we vary the unimodular
F(R)

gravity action (
255
) with
respect to the metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
, we obtain the
unimodular
F(R)
gravity field equations as follows

\displaystyle 0=\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\left(F(R)-\lambda\right)-R_{\mu\nu}F^{\prime}(R)+\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}F^{\prime}(R)-g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{2}F^{\prime}(R)+\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}\,.

(257)

Now, in order to proceed with the cosmological evolution, one needs to be very careful
because the flat standard FLRW metric (
3
)
does not satisfy the unimodular constraint given in
Eq. (
256
). Nevertheless, taking
the following coordinate transformation

\displaystyle d\tau=a^{3}(t)dt\,,

(258)

one can verify that the resulting metric satisfies the unimodular condition (
256
).
Now, with the use of the transformation (
258
), the FLRW metric in (
3
) can be written as

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-a^{-6}\left(t\left(\tau\right)\right)d\tau^{2}+a^{2}\left(t\left(\tau\right)\right)\left(dx^{2}+dy^{2}+dz^{2}\right),

(259)

which for the sake of brevity we call the unimodular FLRW metric.
Using this unimodular FLRW metric, the vacuum field equations become
Nojiri:2015sfd
;
Nojiri:2016ppu
;
Nojiri:2016ygo
;
Odintsov:2016imq
,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{a^{-6}}{2}\left(F(R)-\lambda\right)+\left(3\dot{K}+12K^{2}\right)F^{\prime}(R)-3K\frac{dF^{\prime}(R)}{d\tau}\,,

(260)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\frac{a^{-6}}{2}\left(F(R)-\lambda\right)-\left(\dot{K}+6K^{2}\right)F^{\prime}(R)+5K\frac{dF^{\prime}(R)}{d\tau}+\frac{d^{2}F^{\prime}(R)}{d\tau^{2}}\,,

(261)

where the function
K(\tau)
is defined as the corresponding Hubble rate in the “
\tau
” coordinate, that is,

\displaystyle K=\frac{1}{a(\tau)}\frac{da(\tau)}{d\tau}\,.

(262)

By using the unimodular FLRW metric of
Eq. (
259
), the corresponding Ricci scalar now
becomes

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=a^{6}\left(6\dot{K}+30K^{2}\right)\,.

(263)

Now, in the following sections we describe the correspondence of the Jordan frame unimodular
F(R)
gravity in the Einstein frame.

We begin with the unimodular
F(R)
gravity action in the Jordan
frame, i.e., Eq. (
255
)
without the matter sector, and following the same approach as in
the ordinary
F(R)
gravity, that means, introducing the
auxiliary field
A
, we rewrite the action
(
255
) as follows

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\Bigg[\sqrt{-g}\Bigg(F^{\prime}(A)(R-A)+F(A)-\lambda\Bigg)+\lambda\Bigg]\,.

(264)

We note that the last term of the action
(
264
) will remain
unaffected by the conformal transformations. Now to obtain a
minimally coupled scalar-tensor theory, we perform the conformal
transformation as in the standard
F(R)
gravity case, i.e.,

\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{e}^{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}
,
where
\hat{g}
denotes the metric in the Einstein frame, and
g

is the metric in the Jordan frame. Further, the scalar field

\sigma
in terms of the auxiliary field
A
is given by,

\sigma=-\ln F^{\prime}(A)
, hence, the action
(
264
) becomes,

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\left\{\sqrt{-\hat{g}}\left(\hat{R}-\frac{3}{2}\hat{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\sigma\partial_{\nu}\sigma-V(\sigma)-\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-2\sigma}\right)+\lambda\right\}\,,

(265)

which describes a canonical scalar field action, in the absence of any matter sector.
We note that the unimodular constraint is not unaffected by the conformal transformation, so in the case it becomes

\displaystyle\sqrt{-\hat{g}}=\mathrm{e}^{2\sigma}\,.

(266)

In effect, the FLRW metric does not satisfy this constraint
identically, so in order to overcome this issue, similar to the
earlier approach, we introduce a new time coordinate
\tilde{\tau}
, which
is related to the cosmic time
\tilde{t}
as follows

\displaystyle d\widetilde{\tau}=\widetilde{a}^{3}\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)\mathrm{e}^{2\sigma\left(~\widetilde{t}~\right)}d\widetilde{t}\,,

(267)

so that the conformally transformed unimodular constraint of Eq.
(
266
) is satisfied and the corresponding
Einstein frame unimodular FLRW metric takes the form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{4\sigma\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)}}{\widetilde{a}^{6}\left(~\widetilde{\tau}~\right)}d\widetilde{\tau}^{2}+\widetilde{a}^{2}\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{3}dx_{i}^{2}\,.

(268)

In summary, in order to transform from one frame to other at the
level of metric, we have the following transformations. The scale
factor transforms as

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(~\widetilde{\tau}~\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\sigma/2}a(\tau)\,,

(269)

where the parameter
\widetilde{\tau}
is related to
\tau
as follows,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{4\sigma\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)}}{\widetilde{a}^{6}\left(~\widetilde{\tau}~\right)}\right)d\widetilde{\tau}^{2}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\sigma}}{a^{6}(\tau)}\right)d\tau^{2}\,.

(270)

Combining Eqs. (
269
) and
(
270
), one can easily observe that the
new time coordinate
\widetilde{\tau}
is the same as the
coordinate
\tau
, i.e.,
\widetilde{\tau}=\tau
.

So far we discuss the unimodular
F(R)
gravity scenario, however,
we also need to discuss the scalar-tensor unimodular gravity. To
proceed, we begin with the following minimally coupled
scalar-tensor action

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\left\{\sqrt{-\hat{g}}\left(\frac{\hat{R}}{2\kappa^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-V(\varphi)-\lambda h(\varphi)\right)+\lambda\right\}\,,

(271)

where
\lambda
is a constant and in addition, for the time being
we have assumed that the determinant of the metric is given by an
arbitrary function of the scalar field but this will be determined
later by the requirement that the action has a Jordan frame. Now,
considering the flat unimodular FLRW metric, for the above action
(
271
), one can write down the
gravitational equations which are nothing but the standard scalar
field cosmological equations with a modified scalar potential
having the form
V(\varphi)+\lambda h(\varphi)
. In particular,
the gravitational equations read

\displaystyle 3\widetilde{K}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}+\Big(V(\varphi)+\lambda h(\varphi)\Big)\widetilde{a}^{-6}(~\widetilde{\tau}~)\,,

(272)

\displaystyle 9\widetilde{K}^{2}+2\dot{\widetilde{K}}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}+\Big(V(\varphi)+\lambda h(\varphi)\Big)\widetilde{a}^{-6}(\widetilde{\tau})\,,

(273)

where
\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{\tau})
denotes the unimodular Hubble parameter in the Einstein frame, explicitly given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{K}(\tau)=\frac{1}{\widetilde{a}(\tau)}\frac{d\widetilde{a}(\tau)}{d\tau}\,.

(274)

Now in order to conformally transform this action (
271
) to a Jordan frame, we apply the following conformal transformation

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}\,.

(275)

This rescaling eliminates the kinetic term from the action (
271
) and recast into the following form

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\Bigg[\sqrt{-g}\bigg\{\mathrm{e}^{\pm\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}{2\kappa^{2}}R-\mathrm{e}^{\pm 2\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\bigg(V(\varphi)+\lambda h(\varphi)\bigg)\bigg\}+\lambda\Bigg]\,.

(276)

Now, varying the action (
276
) with respect to the scalar field
\phi
, which is now just an auxiliary field, we get

\displaystyle R=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\Bigg[4\kappa^{2}\bigg(V(\varphi)+\lambda h(\varphi)\bigg)\pm 2\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\bigg(V^{\prime}(\varphi)+\lambda h^{\prime}(\varphi)\bigg)\Bigg]\,.

(277)

In order to find the Jordan frame unimodular
F(R)
gravity, one
needs to invert Eq. (
277
) to get the scalar
field
\phi(R)
, as a function of
R
only, and so independent of

\lambda
. This directs us to choose

\displaystyle h(\varphi)=\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\varphi}\,.

(278)

Hence, in this case we can invert to find
\varphi=\varphi(R)
, and consequently, the resulting unimodular
F(R)
gravity theory takes the form

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\Bigg[\sqrt{-g}\bigg(F(R)-\lambda\bigg)+\lambda\Bigg],

(279)

where the expression of
F(R)
is given by

\displaystyle F(R)=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi(R)}{2\kappa^{2}}R-\mathrm{e}^{\pm 2\kappa\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi(R)}V(\varphi(R))\,.

(280)

Now, having the correspondence between the Jordan and Einstein
frames, in the following we discuss the correspondence of the
finite-time singularities in the two frames. We again note that
for all the physical quantities in the Einstein frame we use
tilde to differ it from the Jordan frame, and additionally, we
set the gravitational coupling constant to be unity throughout the
discussions, i.e.,
\kappa=1
.

(i) Power law evolution

We consider the power law cosmology characterized by the following
scale factor expressed in terms of
\widetilde{\tau}
(the time
coordinate of the unimodular Einstein frame FLRW
metric (
268
)) as follows

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)=\widetilde{a}_{c}\left(\frac{\widetilde{\tau}}{\widetilde{\tau}_{c}}\right)^{p}\,,

(281)

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{c}
is some fiducial time and
p
is a
positive real number. For this scale factor, the unimodular Hubble
parameter becomes
\widetilde{K}(\tau)=p\widetilde{\tau}^{-1}
.
Thus, one can see that at
\widetilde{\tau}=0
, the unimodular
Hubble rate diverges, and hence, for the power law cosmology in the
Einstein frame, a Big Bang singularity is realized. The above
scale factor is a solution of the Einstein frame Friedmann and Raychaudhuri
equations (
272
)-(
273
)
when the potential has an exponential form. In this case the
scalar field takes the form

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)=\pm\sqrt{2p(1-3p)}\,\log\left(\widetilde{\tau}/\widetilde{\tau}_{c}\right)\,.

(282)

Now, using this solution
(
282
), it is possible to
change all the variables from the Einstein frame to the Jordan
frame with the use of the following conformal transformation

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\hat{g}_{\mu\nu}\,.

(283)

As discussed above, the time coordinate
\tau
in the Jordan frame
is equivalent to the original time coordinate in the Einstein
frame. Thus, using the
transformation (
283
), one can
express scale factor in the Jordan frame as follows

\displaystyle a(\tau)=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\varphi}\widetilde{a}(\widetilde{\tau})\sim\tau^{p\pm\sqrt{\frac{p(1-3p)}{3}}}\,.

(284)

Now, when the minus sign is taken in the conformal transformation,
we see that for
0<p<1/6
the quantity
p-\sqrt{\frac{p(1-3p)}{3}}
is negative, so at
\tau=0
the scale factor diverges,
this means that in the Jordan frame we have a contracting
Universe. For
p=1/6
the Universe becomes static. When
1/6<p\leq 1/3
, in the Jordan frame, the Universe conserves the Big Bang
singularity at
\tau=0
, and finally, for
p>1/3
the exponent in
(
284
) becomes complex, meaning that this
case does not make physical sense.

(ii) The case
F(R)=R^{-n}
in the Jordan Frame

Here we investigate a model starting in the Jordan frame and
conformally transform it to the Einstein frame. Let us consider
the vacuum unimodular
F(R)
gravity having a typical form

F(R)\sim R^{-n}
. In this case, the scale factor evolves as

\displaystyle a(\tau)\sim(\tau_{s}-\tau)^{\frac{1+3n+2n^{2}}{5+10n+6n^{2}}}\,,

(285)

where we have used the
\tau
coordinate of the unimodular FLRW
metric. One can see that a Big Rip singularity occurs in the

\tau
coordinate, if the parameter
n
lies in the range

-1<n<-1/2
. Now, in terms of the original FLRW cosmic time
t
,
the scale factor takes the form

\displaystyle a(t)\sim t^{\frac{(2n+1)(n+1)}{2+n}}\;,

(286)

which shows that a Big Rip singularity in the
\tau
coordinate, appears in the
t
coordinate if
n<-2
, or,
-1<n<-1/2
.

We shall now investigate what happens in the corresponding
Einstein frame scalar-tensor theory. The scalar field
\sigma
of
the conformal factor is given by

\displaystyle\sigma\sim(n+1)\ln R\sim-\left(\frac{2(n+1)(2+n)}{5+10n+6n^{2}}\right)\ln(\tau_s-\tau)\,,

(287)

where the Ricci scalar in the unimodular time parameter is given by

\displaystyle R\sim(\tau_{s}-\tau)^{-2\left(\frac{2+n}{5+10n+6n^{2}}\right)}\,.

(288)

This means that under a conformal transformation, the scale factor transforms as

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\sigma/2}a(\tau),

(289)

where the parameter
\widetilde{\tau}
is the same as the original time coordinate
\tau
, that means,
\widetilde{\tau}=\tau
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\sim(\tau_{s}-\tau)^{\frac{n^{2}-1}{6n^{2}+10n+5}}\,.

(290)

These conditions show that we could have more values of
n
for
which the power of the scale factor is negative. As one can see
that for
-1<n<1
, the power of the scale factor becomes
negative. Thus, we conclude that a Type I singularity will appear
for more values of
n
.

(iii) A singular cosmological model

Here we consider a toy model where the unimodular Hubble parameter in the Einstein frame is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}(~\widetilde{\tau}~)

\displaystyle=f_{s}(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s})^{\alpha}\,,

(291)

where
k_{s}
,
\alpha
are real numbers and

\widetilde{\tau}_{s}
refers to a specific time instant of

\widetilde{\tau}
. For this Hubble parameter, the scale factor
can be given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=a_{s}\exp\left[\frac{f_{s}\left(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s}\right)^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\right]\,.

(292)

where
a_{s}
is a constant.

Now, to convert this Einstein frame solution to the Jordan frame,
one needs to find the scalar field
\varphi
giving rise to such a
solution (
291
). To do this it is essential
to solve the differential equation obtained by subtracting the
unimodular Friedmann equations (
272
) and (
273
) as follows

\displaystyle 0=\dot{\varphi}^{2}+6f_{s}^{2}\left(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s}\right)^{2\alpha}+2f_{s}\alpha\left(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s}\right)^{\alpha-1}\,.

(293)

However, this equation cannot be solved in general for an
arbitrary power of
\alpha
, hence, one needs to find an
approximate solution to the above differential equation. Thus, in
order to proceed, one needs to approximate the solution around the
singularity
\widetilde{\tau}=\widetilde{\tau}_{s}
. This can be
done for two cases as follows: when
\alpha<-1
and the Hubble
rate is that of a Type I singularity, and when
\alpha>-1
and the
other types of singularities are present. For
\alpha<-1
, and
around the singularity, the second term
of (
293
) dominates over the third term
and the differential equation (
293
) is
approximated to be

\displaystyle\dot{\varphi}^{2}+6f_{s}^{2}\left(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s}\right)^{2\alpha}

\displaystyle\sim 0\,.

(294)

In this case, the solution for
\varphi
becomes imaginary, and
hence, such a Hubble rate could only be described by a phantom
scalar field. For such a phantom field, the corresponding Jordan
frame
F(R)
becomes complex and this is unphysical.

However, for
\alpha>-1
, and around the singularity, the third
term of (
293
) dominates over the
second term and therefore the scalar field behaves as

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\widetilde{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\pm\frac{2\sqrt{-2f_{s}\alpha}}{\alpha+1}\left(\widetilde{\tau}-\widetilde{\tau}_{s}\right)^{\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}\,,

(295)

which is real if
-2f_{s}\alpha\geq 0
and nontrivial if
-2f_{s}\alpha\neq 0
.
Thus, in this case one can continue further and conformally transform to the Jordan frame.
Applying the conformal transformation (
275
) and using the fact
that the time coordinate is unchanged, i.e.,

\tau=\widetilde{\tau}
, the scale factor in the Jordan frame reads

\displaystyle a(\tau)\sim a_{s}\exp\left(\frac{3f_{s}(\tau-\tau_{s})^{\alpha+1}\pm 2\sqrt{-3\alpha f_{s}}\;(\tau-\tau_{s})^{\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}}{3(\alpha+1)}\right)\,.

(296)

Now from this scale factor (
296
), one
can describe a variety of finite-time singularities as follows:

•

For
-1<\alpha<1
, a Type III singularity occurs.

•

For
1<\alpha<3
, a Type II singularity occurs.

•

For
3<\alpha
, a Type IV singularity occurs.

In TABLE
2
we show how the
singularities change their types from one frame to
another
4
4

4

We note that in
TABLE
2
, we do not keep
Type I singularity since it only appears when
\alpha<-1
, and hence, we have a phantom scalar field and consequently for such a
phantom scalar field, the corresponding Jordan frame
F(R)

becomes complex and this is unphysical.
. One can observe that the
unimodular
F(R)
case behaves similarly to the standard
F(R)

case. From TABLE
2
, we
observe that a Type II singularity in the Einstein frame is
modified to the more severe Type III singularity in the Jordan
frame. The Type IV singularity in the Einstein frame may become a
more severe Type II singularity in the Jordan frame if the
parameter
\alpha
lies in the range
1<\alpha<3
.

Table 2:
We show the correspondence of finite-time singularities
in the Einstein and Jordan frames, for the cosmological evolution
(
291
) in the Einstein frame for the case

\alpha>-1
.

Singularity in the Einstein Frame

Singularity in the Jordan Frame

Type II

Type III

Type III

Type III

Type IV

Type IV or Type II

2.1. Qualitative Analysis of the Phase structure of Unimodular
F(R)
gravity near Finite-Time Singularities

Here we discuss the qualitative behavior of the dynamical system
corresponding to the vacuum unimodular
F(R)
gravity near the
finite-time singularities. To begin with, we introduce the
following variables
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle x_{1}=-\frac{1}{KF^{\prime}(R)}\frac{dF^{\prime}(R)}{d\tau}\,,\quad x_{2}=-\frac{F(R)}{6K^{2}F^{\prime}(R)a^{6}}\,,\quad x_{3}=\frac{R}{6K^{2}a^{6}}\,,\quad x_{4}=\frac{\lambda}{6a^{6}K^{2}F^{\prime}(R)}\,,

(297)

which allows one to write the Friedmann equation as

\displaystyle x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}=1\,.

(298)

This constraint tells us that in order to describe the dynamics one needs only three variables as the fourth variable can be obtained using the remaining three variables.
Using the above variables, the Raychaudhuri equation becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{F^{\prime}(R)K^{2}}\frac{d^{2}F^{\prime}(R)}{d\tau^{2}}=1+5x_{1}+3x_{2}+x_{3}+3x_{4}\,.

(299)

Now, differentiating the variables as defined in
Eq. (
297
) with respect to a
cosmological time
dN=K(\tau)d\tau
, one gets the following
dynamical system
Bahamonde:2016wmz

\displaystyle x_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,-1+x_{1}^{2}-x_{1}x_{3}-3x_{2}-x_{3}-3x_{4}\,,

\displaystyle x_{2}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,-m+4x_{2}+x_{1}x_{2}-2x_{2}x_{3}+50-16x_{3}\,,

\displaystyle x_{3}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,m+20x_{3}-2x_{3}^{2}-50\,,

\displaystyle x_{4}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,x_{4}(x_{1}-2x_{3}+4)\,,

(300)

where
m=\ddot{K}/K^{3}
. Let us note that from the constraint
Eq. (
298
), in reality, one
only has three dynamical equations in
(
IV.5.2
). In fact, one can disregard the
fourth equation which is a combination of the other three. The
dynamical system, as one can notice, is non-autonomous due to the
existence of the term
m
.
The critical points of the system (
IV.5.2
) have been shown in
TABLE
3
. For an autonomous system

m
should vanish. Now if we consider that

K(\tau)=f_{s}(\tau-\tau_{s})^{\alpha}
with
\alpha<-1
which
corresponds to the Big Rip singularity as
\tau\to\tau_{s}
, then
the parameter
m
is equal to,
m=\frac{(-1+\alpha)\alpha(\tau-\tau_{s})^{-2-2\alpha}}{f_{s}^{2}}\sim 0
,
and consequently, the dynamical system of Eq. (
IV.5.2
) becomes
autonomous near the Big Rip singularity. Thus, one may expect that
finding the fixed points of the dynamical system near the Big Rip
singularity could offer some insights on the non-autonomous
system, however, from TABLE
3
,
one can clearly see that if one sets
m=0
, then
x_{1}
and
x_{2}

become complex, that means the critical points are not real. In
fact, if we assume that
m
is a constant, then in order to have
real critical points one needs
m\gtrsim 17
. This indicates that
the dynamics near the Big Rip singularity behaves very strangely.

Table 3:

IV.5.2
).
