IV.6
F(G)
Gravity

Apart from the popular modified gravity models, namely,
F(R)
and

F(T)
, another very interesting class of modified gravity models
which can explain the late-time cosmic acceleration is the
string-inspired modified Gauss–Bonnet gravity
-
the so called

F(G)
-gravity

Nojiri:2005jg
;
Nojiri:2005am
;
Cognola:2006eg
;
Cognola:2006sp
;
Brevik:2006nh
;
Li:2007jm
;
Boehmer:2009fey
;
Sadeghi:2009hi
;
Elizalde:2010jx
;
MohseniSadjadi:2010pc
;
Garcia:2010xz
;
Banijamali:2012zz
;
Rodrigues:2012qu
;
Bamba:2014mya
;
Momeni:2014bua
;
Huang:2015kca
;
Abdolmaleki:2015iaa
;
Astashenok:2015haa
;
Kusakabe:2015ida
;
Oikonomou:2015qha
;
Carloni:2017ucm
;
Oikonomou:2017ppp
;
Terrucha:2019jpm
;
Inagaki:2019rhm
;
Bahamonde:2019swy

where
F(G)
is any arbitrary function of the Gauss-Bonnet
invariant defined in (
152
). Even though this class of
modified gravity models can explain the late-time accelerating
expansion of the Universe, however, it has been found that they
can lead to finite-time future singularities

Bamba:2008ut
;
Bamba:2010wfw
. In this section, we shall
describe the appearance of finite-time singularities in this class
of modified gravity models. Unfortunately this model includes
ghosts
DeFelice:2009ak
although the ghost-free
modifications have been proposed
Nojiri:2018ouv
;
Nojiri:2019dwl
. In this section, however, we show the structure
of the singularities based on the original models for the purpose
of the illustration.

We start with the action of

F(G)
-gravity which is given by
Nojiri:2005jg

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\bigg(R+F(G)\bigg)+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(301)

In the background of a spatially flat FLRW Universe in (
3
), one can write down the
equation of motion for this gravity as follows

\displaystyle 24H^{3}\dot{F}^{\prime}(G)+6H^{2}+F(G)-GF^{\prime}(G)=

\displaystyle\,2\kappa^{2}\rho\,,

(302)

\displaystyle 8H^{2}\ddot{F}^{\prime}(G)+16H\dot{F}^{\prime}(G)\left(\dot{H}+H^{2}\right)+\left(4\dot{H}+6H^{2}\right)+F(G)-GF^{\prime}(G)=

\displaystyle\,-2\kappa^{2}p\,,

(303)

where the overhead dot stands for the derivative with respect to the cosmic time and the prime denotes the differentiation with respect to
G
. The above two equations (
302
) and (
303
) can also be alternatively represented as

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}\,,\quad p_{\mathrm{eff}}=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)\,,

(304)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are
the effective energy density and pressure of the Universe, respectively.
We note that the energy density and pressure of the
matter sector described by
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}
in
Eq. (
301
) are contained in
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and

p_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Now, using the expressions of
R
and
G
in the FLRW Universe, given by

R=6\left(2H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)
and
G=24H^{2}\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)
,
one can visualize
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
of
Eq. (
304
) as follows:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\left[-F(G)+24H^{2}\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)F^{\prime}(G)-24^{2}H^{4}\left(2\dot{H}^{2}+H\ddot{H}+4H^{2}\dot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(G)\right]+\rho\,,

(305)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\Bigg[F(G)-24H^{2}\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)F^{\prime}(G)+(8\times 24)\;H^{2}\Bigl\{6\dot{H}^{3}+8H\dot{H}\ddot{H}+24\dot{H}^{2}H^{2}+6H^{3}\ddot{H}

\displaystyle\,+8H^{4}\dot{H}+H^{2}\dddot{H}\Bigr\}F^{\prime\prime}(G)+(8\times 24^{2})\;H^{4}\left(2\dot{H}^{2}+H\ddot{H}+4H^{2}\dot{H}\right)^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(G)\Bigg]+p\,,

(306)

Assuming the matter sector with a constant EoS parameter
w\equiv p/\rho
, and then combining the Friedmann and Raychaudhuri
equations in Eq. (
304
), one leads to the following equation:

\displaystyle{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right],

(307)

where

\displaystyle{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)=p_{\mathrm{eff}}-w\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}.

(308)

The Eq. (
307
) brings in a very crucial physical
insight in terms of the new function
{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)
which involves
H
,
\dot{H}
and the
higher order derivatives of
H
. When a cosmological model is
prescribed in terms of the Hubble rate
H=H(t)
, the
right-hand side of Eq. (
307
) then reduces to a
function,
f(t)
of the cosmic time
t
. Now, if the function

{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)
in
Eq. (
308
) is chosen in such a way so that

{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)
reproduces the
above function
f(t)
, then the aforementioned cosmology given by

H=H(t)
can be realized. Therefore, the function

{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)
plays a very
crucial role to judge the viability of a given cosmological
scenario characterized by the Hubble rate
Bamba:2008ut
.

The mathematical form of
{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)
can be determined from the prescribed
gravitational theory. In the context of
F(G)
-gravity, inserting
Eqs. (
305
) and (
306
) into
Eq. (
308
), one obtains,

\displaystyle{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle\,\left[(1+w)F(G)-24(1+w)H^{2}\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)F^{\prime}(G)+(8\times 24)H^{2}\left\{6\dot{H}^{3}+8H\dot{H}\ddot{H}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\,\left.+6(4+w)\dot{H}^{2}H^{2}+3(2+w)H^{3}\ddot{H}+4(2+3w)H^{4}\dot{H}+H^{2}\dddot{H}\right\}F^{\prime\prime}(G)

\displaystyle\,\left.+(8\times 24^{2})H^{4}\left(2\dot{H}^{2}+H\ddot{H}+4H^{2}\dot{H}\right)^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(G)\right]\,.

(309)

IV.6.1
Finite-time Singularities

In this section, we investigate the possibility of finite-time
future singularities in
F(G)
gravity models. To start with we
consider the following expression of the Hubble parameter in (
209
). From
Eq. (
209
) one can clearly notice that if

\beta>0
, the
H(t)
becomes singular in the limit
t\rightarrow t_{s}
.
Therefore,
t_{s}
determines the time when a singularity
appears in the above model. While on the other hand, for

\beta<0
,
H(t)
does not exhibit any singular behavior, but for
non-integer negative values of
\beta
, some derivative of
H(t)

may be singular and consequently the curvature could exhibit
singular behavior
Bamba:2008ut
. Therefore, we see that for
both positive and negative values of
\beta
, singularities in
H(t)
or in the curvature may appear. Therefore, we assume

\beta\neq 0
because the case
\beta=0
corresponds to the de
Sitter space, which has no singularity.

Thus, for the above cosmology characterized by the Hubble rate
in Eq. (
209
), one can try to find the equivalent

F(G)
models following the reconstruction technique described in
Refs.
Nojiri:2006su
;
Nojiri:2006gh
;
Bamba:2008ut
;
Bamba:2010wfw
.
Now, with the choice of suitable functions
P(t)
and
Q(t)
of a
scalar field
t
which in this case is identified with the cosmic
time, the action integral in Eq. (
301
) can be
expressed as

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\bigg(R+P(t)G+Q(t)\bigg)+{{L}}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,.

(310)

The variation of the action in Eq. (
310
)
with respect to
t
gives,

\displaystyle\frac{dP(t)}{dt}G+\frac{dQ(t)}{dt}=0\,,

(311)

from which one can in principle express
t=t(G)
. Now,
substituting
t=t(G)
into Eq. (
310
), one
can express the action in terms of
F(G)
where

\displaystyle F(G)=P\left(t(G)\right)G+Q\left(t(G)\right)\,.

(312)

Now, subtracting the equations of motion, namely,
Eqs. (
302
) and (
303
), we obtain a second
order differential equation

\displaystyle 8\frac{d}{dt}\left(H^{2}\frac{dP}{dt}\right)-8H^{3}\frac{dP}{dt}+4\dot{H}+2\kappa^{2}\rho_{0}(1+w)a^{-3(1+w)}=0\,,

(313)

where
\rho_{0}
refers to the present energy density of
\rho
.
We note that instead of considering only one fluid in Eqs.
(
305
) and (
306
), if one would consider
several matter components with
w_{i}=p_{i}/\rho_{i}
being the EoS
parameter of the
i
-th fluid, then in this case, the last term of
the equation (
313
) will be replaced by
2\kappa^{2}\sum_{i}(1+w_{i})\rho_{i0}a^{-3(1+w_{i})}
. If one can
solve for
P(t)
from Eq. (
313
), then from
Eq. (
302
),
one can find
Q(t)
as follows

\displaystyle Q(t)=-24H^{3}\frac{dP}{dt}-6H^{2}+2\kappa^{2}\rho_{0}a^{-3(1+w)}\,.

(314)

Thus, one can see that any cosmology which is prescribed in terms of any Hubble rate,
then this cosmology can be realized by some specific
F(G)

gravity model. Just for simplicity, we neglect the matter term
from the above equations and focus on the specific
F(G)
models
described by the Hubble rate given in
Eq. (
209
). Therefore, under the absence of
matter term, Eqs. (
313
) and
(
314
) become,

\displaystyle 8\frac{d}{dt}\left(H\frac{dP}{dt}\right)-8H^{3}\frac{dP}{dt}+4\dot{H}=0\,,

(315)

and

\displaystyle Q(t)=-24H^{3}\frac{dP}{dt}-6H^{2}\,.

(316)

In the following we show a correspondence between the finite-time
singularities and the associated
F(G)
model.

(i)
Big Rip singularity:

Let us focus on the Big Rip singularity which is realized by some
of the
F(G)
gravity models. To begin with we consider
\beta=1

and
H_{s}=0
in Eq. (
209
), for which the
Hubble rate
H(t)
takes the form of (
61
)
Bamba:2010wfw

and consequently one can derive
G
as follows,

\displaystyle G=\frac{24h_{s}^{3}}{(t_{s}-t)^{4}}(1+h_{s})\,.

(317)

Thus, with the above choice of
H
, the
most general solution of the second order differential equation (
315
) is
Bamba:2010wfw

\displaystyle P(t)=\frac{1}{4h_{s}(h_{s}-1)}(2t_{s}-t)t+c_{1}\frac{(t_{s}-t)^{3-h_{s}}}{3-h_{s}}+c_{2}\,,

(318)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are arbitrary constants. Having the
solution for
P(t)
as in Eq. (
318
), from
Eq. (
316
), one can derive
Q(t)
as

Bamba:2010wfw

\displaystyle Q(t)=-\frac{6h_{s}^{2}}{(t_{s}-t)^{2}}-\frac{24h_{s}^{3}}{(t_{s}-t)^{3}}\left[\frac{(t_{s}-t)}{2h_{s}(h_{s}-1)}-c_{1}(t_{s}-t)^{2-h_{s}}\right]\,.

(319)

Further, from Eq. (
311
) we obtain

\displaystyle t-t_{s}=\left[\dfrac{24(h_{s}^{3}+h_{s}^{4})}{G}\right]^{1/4}\,,

(320)

which, as one can see, is consistent with
Eq. (
317
). Thus, having all the above information,
using Eq. (
312
), one can write down the most general
form of
F(G)
realizing the Big Rip singularity as follows

Bamba:2010wfw
:

\displaystyle F(G)=\left(\frac{\sqrt{6h_{s}^{3}(1+h_{s})}}{h_{s}(1-h_{s})}\right)\sqrt{G}+c_{1}G^{\frac{1+h_{s}}{4}}+c_{2}G\,.

(321)

For
h_{s}=1
, that means when the Hubble rate becomes
H(t)=(t_{s}-t)^{-1}
,
one can also find
another exact solution for
P(t)
as follows,

\displaystyle P(t)=\alpha(t_{s}-t)^{q}\ln\left[\gamma(t_{s}-t)^{z}\right]\,,

(322)

where
\gamma
is a positive real number,
From Eq. (
316
), one can derive,

\displaystyle Q(t)=-\frac{12}{(t_{s}-t)^{2}}\ln\left[\gamma(t_{s}-t)\right]\,,

(323)

Thus, using the expressions for
P(t)
and
Q(t)
,
the form
of
F(G)
is given by
Bamba:2010wfw

\displaystyle F(G)=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\sqrt{G}\ln(\gamma G)\,.

(324)

This form of
F(G)
corresponds to the Hubble rate (
209
) with
h_{s}=1
and
H_{s}=0
and it realizes the Big Rip singularity. In general, for large values of
G
,
F(G)\sim\alpha\sqrt{G}\ln(\gamma G)
with
\alpha>0
,
\gamma>0
, and the Big Rip singularity may appear. Moreover, the Big Rip singularity may also appear for
F(G)\sim\alpha\sqrt{G}\ln(\gamma G^z + G_s)
with
\alpha>0
,
\gamma>0
,
z>0
where
G_{s}
is a constant such that
\gamma G^{z}+G_{s}>0
.

(ii)
Other types of singularities:

Let us now discuss the appearance of other types of singularities
in
F(G)
-gravity models. We consider the Hubble rate of
Eq. (
209
) with
H_{s}=0
but
\beta\neq 1
. In
this case, the evolution of the scale factor
a(t)
turns out to
be

\displaystyle a(t)=a_{0}\exp\left[\frac{h_{s}}{\beta-1}\left((t_{s}-t)^{1-\beta}-(t_{s}-t_{0})^{1-\beta}\right)\right]\,,

(325)

and we observe the emergence of following singularities:

•

When
\beta>1
, then
H
and
G
are given by

\displaystyle H=\frac{h_{s}}{(t_{s}-t)^{\beta}}\,,\quad G\sim\frac{24h_{s}^{4}}{(t_{s}-t)^{4\beta}}\,.

(326)

A solution of the Eq. (
315
) in the limit

t\rightarrow t_{s}
can be found as

\displaystyle P(t)\simeq\frac{\alpha}{(t_{s}-t)^{z}}\,,

(327)

where
z=-2\beta
and
\alpha=-1/4h_{s}^{2}
. The expression of
F(G)
now follows

\displaystyle F(G)=-12\sqrt{\frac{G}{24}}\,.

(328)

Hence, if for large values of
G
,
F(G)\sim-\alpha\sqrt{G}

with
\alpha>0
, then a Type I singularity could appear.

•

When
0<\beta<1
for which
the forms of
H
and
G
are given by

\displaystyle H=\frac{h_{s}}{(t_{s}-t)^{\beta}}\,,\quad G\sim\frac{24h_{s}^{3}\beta}{(t_{s}-t)^{3\beta+1}}\,.

(329)

In this case, an asymptotic solution of
Eq. (
315
) in the limit
t\rightarrow t_{s}
can be found as

\displaystyle P(t)\simeq\frac{\alpha}{(t_{s}-t)^{z}}\;,

(330)

where
z=-(1+\beta)
and
\alpha=1/2h_{s}(1+\beta)
. The form of
F(G)

now becomes

\displaystyle F(G)=\frac{6h_{s}^{2}}{(\beta+1)}(3\beta+1)\left(\frac{|G|}{24h_{s}^{3}|\beta|}\right)^{2\beta/(3\beta+1)}\,.

(331)

Hence, if for large values of
G
,
F(G)
has the following form

\displaystyle F(G)\sim\alpha|G|^{\gamma}\,,\quad\gamma=\frac{2\beta}{3\beta+1}\,,

(332)

where
\alpha>0
and
0<\gamma<1/2
, then
because we are assuming
0<\beta<1
and in that case, a Type III singularity could emerge.

Finally, for a general value of
\beta
we have the following:

–

If for
G\rightarrow-\infty
,
F(G)
has the form as in
Eq. (
332
) with
\alpha>0
and

-\infty<\gamma<0
, then we find
-1/3<\beta<0
and a Type II
(sudden) singularity could appear.

–

If for
G\rightarrow 0^{-}
,
F(G)
takes the form of
Eq. (
332
) with
\alpha<0
and

1<\gamma<\infty
, then we obtain
-1<\beta<-1/3
and a Type II
singularity could occur.

–

If for
G\rightarrow 0^{-}
,
F(G)
assumes the form as in
Eq. (
332
) with
\alpha>0
and

2/3<\gamma<1
, then we obtain
-\infty<\beta<-1
and a Type IV
singularity could appear. We also require that

\gamma\neq 2n/(3n-1)
, i.e.,
\beta\not=n
, where
n
is a natural
number.

Let us note that we can generate all the possible Type II singularities
as shown above except for
\beta=-1/3
,
i.e., for
H=h_{s}(t_{s}-t)^{1/3}
because
\gamma=0
. In this case,
G
takes the form:

\displaystyle G=24h_{s}^{3}\beta+24h_{s}^{4}(t_{s}-t)^{4/3}<0\,.

(333)

Thus, in order to express
t
in terms of
G
, it is essential to consider the whole expression of
G
, and by considering the leading term involving
(t_{s}-t)
in (
313
) and (
314
) with
\rho_{0}=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle F(G)\simeq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{6}h_{s}^{3}}G(G+8h_{s}^{3})^{1/2}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}(G+8h_{s}^{3})^{1/2}\,,

(334)

which satisfies Eq. (
307
) in the limit

t\rightarrow t_{s}
. Consequently, the specific model
F(G)=\sigma_{1}G(G+c_{3})^{1/2}+\sigma_{2}(G+c_{3})^{1/2}
, where

\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
c_{3}
are positive constants, can
generate a Type II singularity.

IV.7
F(R,G)
Gravity

The
F(R,G)
-gravity is a very generalized gravitational theory
where
F
is a generalized function of the Ricci scalar
R

and the Gauss-Bonnet invariant is defined in Eq. (
152
). One can
clearly see that the
F(R,G)
gravity theory can recover

F(R)
-gravity and
F(G)
-gravity as special cases. Moreover, with
the suitable choice for
F
leading to
F(R,G)=R
, one
recovers Einstein’s GR as well. Thus, one can see that this
modified gravity theory being the generalized version of both
F(R)
and
F(G)
gravity theories can offer some appealing consequences and very
soon of its introduction,
F(R,G)
gravity theory received
significant attention in the community

Bamba:2010wfw
;
Elizalde:2010jx
;
DeFelice:2010hg
;
DeFelice:2011ka
;
delaCruz-Dombriz:2011oii
;
Makarenko:2012gm
;
Atazadeh:2013cz
;
SantosDaCosta:2018bbw
;
Wu:2018jve
;
Odintsov:2018nch
;
Camci:2018apx
;
Barros:2019pvc
;
KumarSanyal:2019rpa
;
Shekh:2019rib
;
Inagaki:2019rhm
;
Elizalde:2020zcb
;
Navo:2020eqt
;
Odintsov:2021nim
;
Lohakare:2021yuo
;
Nojiri:2021mxf
.
In the present section we shall investigate the finite-time future
singularities appearing in this generalized modified gravitational
theory.
This model includes ghosts
DeFelice:2009ak
and the ghost-free modifications
have been proposed in Ref.
Nojiri:2021mxf
.
In this section, however, again for the illustration, we show the structure of the singularities based on the original models.

The action of the
{F}(R,G)
-gravity is given by

Bamba:2010wfw
;
delaCruz-Dombriz:2011oii
;
Atazadeh:2013cz
:

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{{F}(R,G)}{2\kappa^{2}}+{{L}}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,,

(335)

For the action (
335
), one can derive the gravitational equations as follows

\displaystyle{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\kappa^{2}T^{(\mathrm{matter})}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\left({F}-{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}R\right)+{\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-g_{\mu\nu}\Box{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}

\displaystyle\,+\left(-2RR_{\mu\nu}+4R_{\mu\rho}R_{\nu}{}^{\rho}-2R_{\mu}{}^{\rho\sigma\tau}R_{\nu\rho\sigma\tau}+4g^{\alpha\rho}g^{\beta\sigma}R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}R_{\rho\sigma}\right){{F}}^{\prime}_{G}

\displaystyle\,+2\left({\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R-2g_{\mu\nu}\left(\Box{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R+4\left(\Box{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R_{\mu\nu}-4\left({\nabla}_{\rho}{\nabla}_{\mu}{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R_{\nu}{}^{\rho}

\displaystyle\,-4\left({\nabla}_{\rho}{\nabla}_{\nu}{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R_{\mu}{}^{\rho}+4g_{\mu\nu}\left({\nabla}_{\rho}{\nabla}_{\sigma}{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)R^{\rho\sigma}-4\left({\nabla}_{\rho}{\nabla}_{\sigma}{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\right)g^{\alpha\rho}g^{\beta\sigma}R_{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}\,,

(336)

where
{\nabla}_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative
operator associated with the metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
;

\Box\equiv g^{\mu\nu}{\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}

denotes the covariant d’Alembertian for a scalar field;

T^{(\mathrm{matter})}_{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{diag}\left(\rho,p,p,p\right)
is
the energy-momentum tensor of the matter sector that includes all the ordinary matter fluids
with
\rho~(=\sum_{i}\rho_{i})
and
p~(=\sum_{i}p_{i})
are the total energy density
and pressure of all the fluids, respectively
(
\rho_{i}
,
p_{i}
being the representatives of the
i
-th fluid), and

\displaystyle{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}=\frac{\partial{F}(R,G)}{\partial R}\,,\quad{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}=\frac{\partial{F}(R,G)}{\partial G}\,.

(337)

In the background of a spatially flat FLRW Universe (
3
), the
gravitational field equations in
(
336
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}\,,\quad p_{\mathrm{eff}}=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)\,,

(338)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
, termed as the
effective energy density and pressure of the Universe,
respectively, are given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}}\left[\rho+\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\left\{\left({{F}}^{\prime}_{R}R-{F}\right)-6H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}-24H^{3}{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}\right\}\right]\,,

(339)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}}\left[p+\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\left\{-\left({{F}}^{\prime}_{R}R-{F}\right)+4H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+2{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}+16H\left(\dot{H}+H^{2}\right){\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}+8H^{2}{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}\right\}\right]\,.

(340)

Let us now proceed towards the investigations of the finite-time
singularities in the
{F}(R,G)
-gravity. Similar to the earlier
section
IV.6
on
F(G)
, we reconstruct the

{F}(R,G)
-gravity models that can produce finite-time
singularities. In order to do so, we consider the pure
gravitation action of
{F}(R,G)
-gravity, that means action
(
335
) without matter sector exactly what we have
considered in the earlier section
IV.6
. In this
case, from Eqs. (
339
) and
(
340
), one can write down the gravitational
equations as

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,24H^{3}{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}+6H^{2}{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}+6H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+({F}-R{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G})\,,

(341)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,8H^{2}{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}+2{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+4H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+16H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}(\dot{H}+H^{2})+{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}(4\dot{H}+6H^{2})+{F}-R{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\,.

(342)

We note that in the case of pure gravity (i.e., when no matter sector is present in the gravitational action),
the above equations (
341
) and (
342
) are linearly dependent.

Now, following the similar fashion as adopted in the earlier
section (
IV.6
), with the choice of the proper
functions
P(t)
,
Z(t)
and
Q(t)
of a scalar field (where we
identify the scalar field with time
t
), one can rewrite
Eq. (
335
) without
{{L}}_{\mathrm{matter}}
as

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\Bigg(P(t)R+Z(t)G+Q(t)\Bigg)\,.

(343)

By varying the action in Eq. (
310
) with
respect to
t
, we obtain

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(t)R+Z^{\prime}(t)G+Q^{\prime}(t)=0\,,

(344)

where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
t
. From
Eq. (
344
), one can in principle express
t

as a function of
R,G
, i.e.,
t=t(R,G)
. Now, with the
substitution of
t=t(R,G)
in Eq. (
310
),
we can express
{F}(R,G)
as

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=P\left(t(R,G)\right)R+Z\left(t(R,G)\right)G+Q\left(t(R,G)\right)\,.

(345)

IV.7.1
Finite-time Singularities

We consider, once again, the following expression of the Hubble parameter in (
209
)
Bamba:2010wfw
.
From Eq. (
209
), one can express the scale factor as

\displaystyle a(t)=\bar{a}\exp\left(g(t)\right)\,,

(346)

where
\bar{a}
is a constant and
g(t)
is some differentiable
function of
t
satisfying
\dot{g}(t)=H(t)
. Using the
conservation law and Eq. (
341
), we arrive
at the following second order differential equation

\displaystyle P^{\prime\prime}(t)+4\dot{g}^{2}(t)Z^{\prime\prime}(t)-\dot{g}(t)P^{\prime}(t)+(8\dot{g}\ddot{g}-4\dot{g}^{3}(t))Z^{\prime}(t)+2\ddot{g}(t)P(t)=0\,,

(347)

where we have implemented the expression of the scale factor as
given in Eq. (
346
). Now, from
Eq. (
341
), one can derive
Q(t)
as

\displaystyle Q(t)=-24\dot{g}^{3}(t)Z^{\prime}(t)-6\dot{g}^{2}(t)P(t)-6\dot{g}(t)P^{\prime}(t)\,.

(348)

If
P(t)\neq 0
, then
{F}(R,G)
can be written as

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=R\;\psi(R,G)+f(R,G)\,,

(349)

where
\psi(R,G)\neq 0
and
f(R,G)
is any generic function of
R
and
G
.
Now, from Eqs. (
339
) and
(
340
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}g(R,G)}\biggl[24H^{3}{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}+6H^{2}\left(R\frac{d\psi(R,G)}{dR}+\frac{df(R,G)}{dR}\right)+6H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+({F}-R{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G})\biggr],

(350)

and

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}g(R,G)}\biggl[8H^{2}{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}+2{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+4H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+16H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}(\dot{H}+H^{2})

\displaystyle\,+\left(R\frac{d\psi(R,G)}{dR}+\frac{df(R,G)}{dR}\right)(4\dot{H}+6H^{2})+{F}-R{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G}\biggr]\,,

(351)

and similar to the earlier Section
IV.6
, the function
{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)
can be written as

\displaystyle{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},\dddot{H},...\right)=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right],

(352)

where more explicitly,

\displaystyle{G}(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...)=

\displaystyle\,p_{\mathrm{eff}}-w\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}\psi(R,G)}\Bigg[(1+w)({F}-R{{F}}^{\prime}_{R}-G{{F}}^{\prime}_{G})+\left(R\frac{d\psi(R,G)}{dR}+\frac{df(R,G)}{dR}\right)\left(6H^{2}(1+w)+4\dot{H}\right)

\displaystyle\,+H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}(4+6w)+8H{\dot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}\left(2\dot{H}+H^{2}(2+3w)\right)+2{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{R}+8H^{2}{\ddot{{F}}}^{\prime}_{G}\Bigg]\,,

(353)

where
w
is the EoS parameter of the matter. It is important to note that the use of the above equation (
353
) demands
\psi(R,G)\neq 0
on the solution.
Let us now explicitly describe the finite-time singularities
allowed in various
{F}(R,G)
-gravity models.

(i) Big Rip singularity

Let us first discuss the appearance of the Big Rip singularity in
this modified gravity model. We consider the Hubble rate of
Eq. (
209
) with
\beta=1
and
H_{s}=0
which
gives the Hubble rate in (
61
)
and consequently, one can derive
R
and
G
for
Eq. (
61
) as

\displaystyle R=\frac{6h_{s}}{(t_{s}-t)^{2}}(2h_{s}+1)\,,\quad G=\frac{24h_{s}^{3}}{(t_{s}-t)^{4}}(1+h_{s})\,.

(354)

A very trivial solution of Eq. (
347
) can be given by

\displaystyle P(t)=\alpha(t_{s}-t)^{z}\,,\quad Z(t)=\delta(t_{s}-t)^{x}\,,

(355)

where
\alpha
and
\delta
be any real numbers;
x=3-h_{s}
, and
z
can be found from

\displaystyle z_{\pm}=\frac{1-h_{s}\pm\sqrt{h_{s}^{2}-10h_{s}+1}}{2}\,.

(356)

Therefore, the most general solution of
P(t)
can be given as

\displaystyle P(t)=\alpha_{1}(t_{s}-t)^{z_{+}}+\alpha_{2}(t_{s}-t)^{z_{-}}\,,

(357)

where
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are any real numbers. Now,
from Eq. (
348
), one can derive

\displaystyle Q(t)=\dfrac{24h_{s}^{3}\delta(3-h_{s})}{(t_{s}-t)^{h_{s}+1}}+\dfrac{6h_{s}\alpha_{1}(z_{+}-h_{s})}{(t_{s}-t)^{2-z_{+}}}+\dfrac{6h_{s}\alpha_{2}(z_{-}-h_{s})}{(t_{s}-t)^{2-z_{-}}}\,.

(358)

Now for the condition
0<h_{s}<5-2\sqrt{6}
or
h_{s}>2+\sqrt{6}
, the solution of

{F}(R,G)
is given by

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\bar{\alpha}_{1}R^{1-\frac{z_{+}}{2}}+\bar{\alpha}_{2}R^{1-\frac{z_{-}}{2}}+\bar{\delta}G^{\frac{h_{s}+1}{4}}\,,

(359)

where some factors have been absorbed into the constants. Note also that
z_{\pm}\neq 0
, otherwise
h_{s}
vanishes which is a contradiction.

Another solution of Eq. (
347
) can be given by

\displaystyle P(t)=\frac{\alpha}{(t_{s}-t)^{z}}\,,\quad Z(t)=\frac{\delta}{(t_{s}-t)^{x}}\,,

(360)

where
\delta
,
x
are real numbers;
z=x+2
and
\alpha
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha=\dfrac{4h_{s}^{2}\delta x(h_{s}-x-3)}{x^{2}+(5-h_{s})x+6}\,.

(361)

From Eq. (
348
), one finds

\displaystyle Q(t)=-\frac{6h_{s}}{(t_{s}-t)^{x+4}}\left[4h_{s}^{2}\delta x+\alpha(x+2+h_{s})\right]\,.

(362)

The solution of the Eq. (
344
) is given by

\displaystyle t=t(R,G)=t_{s}-\left[\;\;\dfrac{-\alpha(x+2)R\pm\sqrt{\alpha^{2}(x+2)^{2}R^{2}+24h_{s}\bigl(4h_{s}^{2}\delta x+\alpha(x+2+h_{s})\bigr)(x+4)\delta xG}}{2\delta xG}\;\;\right]^{1/2}\,,

(363)

where
x\neq 0
and
\delta\neq 0
. In order to get real
solutions, one has to ensure that first of all, we should have

\Delta=\alpha^{2}(x+2)^{2}R^{2}+24h_{s}\bigl(4h_{s}^{2}\delta x+\alpha(x+2+h_{s})\bigr)(x+4)\geq 0
in
Eq. (
363
) and secondly, the entire term
inside the third brace of Eq. (
363
) has
to be positive. For
h_{s}>0
, the principal cases with the real
solutions of (
363
) are the following:

•

Case I: For
x>0
,
\delta>0
,
1+x\leq h_{s}<x+5+\frac{6}{x}
, we need to use the sign
+
in the r.h.s. of Eq. (
363
).

•

Case II: For
-\frac{3}{2}\leq x<0
,
\delta<0
,
h_{s}\geq x+1
, we need to use the sign
+
in the r.h.s. of
Eq. (
363
).

•

Case III: For
-4<x<-\frac{3}{2}
,
\delta<0
,

h_{s}>x+5+\frac{6}{x}
, we must use the sign
+
in the r.h.s. of Eq. (
363
).

•

Case IV: For
x>0
,
\delta<0
,
x+5+\frac{6}{x}>h_{s}\geq 1+x
, we need to use the sign
-
in the r.h.s. of
Eq. (
363
).

•

Case V: For
-\frac{3}{2}\leq x<0
,
\delta>0
,
h_{s}\geq x+1
, we must use the sign
-
in the r.h.s. of
Eq. (
363
).

•

Case VI: For
-4<x<-\frac{3}{2}
,
\delta>0
,

h_{s}>x+5+\frac{6}{x}
, we need to use the sign
-
in the right
hand side of Eq. (
363
).

•

Case VII: For
x=-4
,
\delta>0
, we must use the sign
-

in the r.h.s. of Eq. (
363
).

•

Case VIII: For
x=-4
,
\delta<0
, we must use the sign
+

in the r.h.s. of Eq. (
363
).

The solution of
{F}(R,G)
is then given by

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\left(\frac{\alpha}{(t_{s}-t(R,G))^{x+2}}\right)R+\left(\frac{\delta}{(t_{s}-t(R,G))^{x}}\right)G-\left(\frac{6h_{s}}{(t_{s}-t(R,G))^{x+4}}\right)\Bigg[4h^{2}\delta x+\alpha(x+2+h_{s})\Bigg]\,,

(364)

where
t(R,G)
is given by Eq. (
363
).
The expression for
{F}(R,G)
in Eq. (
364
)
represents an exact solution of the equation of motion in
Eqs. (
339
) and (
340
)
allowing the Big Rip singularity. Let us show some specific
examples of
{F}(R,G)
-gravity models allowing the Big Rip
singularity obtained for some specific values of
\alpha
and
x
.

•

For
\alpha=1
and
x=-2
, we find

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=R+\left(\dfrac{\sqrt{6}\sqrt{h_{s}(1+h_{s})}}{(1-h_{s})}\right)\sqrt{G}\,,\quad h\neq 1\,.

(365)

•

If
\alpha=0
and
x=h_{s}-3
(this case corresponds to Case (I)
-
Case (VI), presented above), then one finds

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\delta\;G^{\frac{h_{s}+1}{4}}\,,\quad\delta\neq 0\,,

(366)

which is actually equivalent to Eq. (
359
) for

\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=0
.

•

If
x=-4
, then the model reduces to

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\dfrac{16h_{s}^{4}\delta}{(1+2h_{s}^{2})^{2}}\left[(9+21h_{s}+6h_{s}^{2})-(1+h_{s})^{2}\frac{R^{2}}{G}\right]\,,\quad\delta\neq 0\,.

(367)

Thus, we see that for large values of
R
and
G
,

{F}(R,G)\sim\pm\alpha\mp\delta(R^{2}/G)
with

\alpha>0
and
\delta>0
, then the Big Rip singularity could appear.

•

For
x=h_{s}-1
, the model
becomes (by absorbing some constants)

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\delta\left(\dfrac{R}{G}\right)^{\frac{1-h_{s}}{2}}G\,,\quad\delta\neq 0\,,\quad h_{s}\neq 1\,.

(368)

Thus, we see that for large values of
R
and
G
,

{F}(R,G)\sim\delta G^{\gamma}/R^{\gamma-1}

with
\delta\neq 0
and
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
or
1<\gamma<+\infty
,
and the Big Rip singularity could appear.

We close the discussion with a general model
that allows Big Rip singularity.
One can verify that the model

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\gamma\frac{G^{m}}{R^{n}}\,,

(369)

with
\gamma
being any real number, is a solution of
Eqs. (
339
) and (
340
) in the
case of the Big Rip singularity for some value of
h_{s}
. In
general, it is possible to obtain solutions for
h_{s}>0
if
m>0
,

n>0
and
m>n
. For example, the case
n=2
and
m=3
realizes
the Big Rip singularity in
h_{s}=5
; the case
n=1
and
m=3

realizes the big rip singularity in
h_{s}=4+\sqrt{19}
and so on.
This is a generalization of Eq. (
368
). It is
important here to mention that for
m=-1
and
n=-2
, we do not
recover a physical scenario because in this case we obtain

h_{s}=-3
. However, this case (i.e.,
m=-1
and
n=-2
), has a
similarity with Eq. (
367
) allowing the Big Rip
singularity. Finally, for the case with
m=0
or
n=0
, we recover
the model in Eq. (
359
).

(ii) Other types of singularities

Here we discuss other types of singularities that may appear in various
{F}(R,G)
-gravity models.
To begin with we consider the following expansion of the Hubble rate in (
107
).
For the above choice of the Hubble rate, an exact solution of
the differential Eq. (
347
) can be found as

\displaystyle P(t)=-\lambda(4h_{s}^{2})(t_{s}-t)\,,\quad Z(t)=\lambda(t_{s}-t)^{2\beta+1}\,,

(370)

where
\lambda
is a constant. The form of
Q(t)
from
Eq. (
348
) can now be derived as

\displaystyle Q(t)=\dfrac{24h_{s}^{4}\lambda}{(t_{s}-t)^{2\beta-1}}+\dfrac{48h_{s}^{3}\beta}{(t_{s}-t)^{\beta}}\,.

(371)

Now we examine the cases when
\beta>1
or
\beta<1
.

•

For
\beta>1
, one may obtain the asymptotic real solution
of Eq. (
344
):

\displaystyle t=t(R,G)=t_{s}-2^{1/2\beta}\left[\dfrac{h_{s}^{2}R+\sqrt{h_{s}^{4}R^{2}+6h_{s}^{4}(4\beta^{2}-1)G}}{(1+2\beta)G}\right]^{1/2\beta}\,.

(372)

Thus, the mathematical form of
{F}(R,G)
can now be expressed as

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=-4h_{s}^{2}\lambda(t_{s}-t(R,G))R+\lambda(t_{s}-t(R,G))^{1+2\beta}G+24h_{s}^{4}\lambda(t_{s}-t(R,G))^{1-2\beta}\,,\quad\beta>1\,.

(373)

This is an asymptotic solution of
Eq. (
352
) for
\beta>1
, when

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right]\sim-\dfrac{3(1+w)h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}(t_{s}-t)^{-2\beta}\,.

(374)

Interestingly, for
\beta\gg 1
, the mathematical form of
{F}(R,G)
is given by

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)\simeq\lambda\left(\dfrac{\alpha G}{R+\sqrt{R^{2}+\gamma G}}-R\right)\,,\quad\alpha>0\,,\quad\gamma>0\,,\quad\lambda\neq 0\,,

(375)

and this is the asymptotic behavior of a
{F}(R,G)
model where
a “strong” Type I singularity (
\beta\gg 1
) may appear.

We consider some other explicit cases where Type I singularity appears as follows.
Taking

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=\gamma\frac{G^{m}}{R^{n}}\,,

(376)

and using Eqs. (
339
) and
(
340
), one can verify that
the function
{G}\left(H,\dot{H},\ddot{H},...\right)
in
Eq. (
353
) becomes,

\displaystyle{G}(H,\dot{H},..)\simeq-\frac{3h_{s}^{2}(2m-n-1)(1+w)}{\kappa^{2}(t_{s}-t)^{2\beta}}\,,

(377)

which under the restriction
2m-n-1>0
, represents an asymptotic
solution of Eq. (
352
) for
\beta>1
.
Thus, we see that for the model
{F}(R,G)\simeq\gamma G^{m}/R^{n}
with
m>(n+1)/2
, the Type I singularity may appear.
This observation has some important consequences in the context of
other modified gravity models because one can see that the
modified gravity models where either
F(R)=R^{n}
with
n>1
or

F(G)=G^{m}
with
m>1/2
can allow singularities.

One can also find some other models allowing the Type I singularities and we can construct these models following section
IV.6
as follows.
The Type I singularities actually correspond to the asymptotic limits for
R
and
G

\displaystyle R\sim 12H^{2}\,,\quad G\sim 24H^{4}\,.

(378)

Since
R
and
H
in the asymptotic limits are nothing but the functions of the Hubble parameter only, therefore, one can write that,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{s}}24\left(\frac{R}{12}\right)^{2}=\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{s}}G\,.

(379)

Now, if we substitute
G
for
R
in Eq. (
328
) considering
Eq. (
379
), then we obtain a zero function
5
5

5

The
reason for obtaining the zero function is that, in this case
Eq. (
328
) becomes zero on the singularity solution.
.
Thus, if we instead substitute
G
for
G/R
, we therefore obtain
the following model

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=R-\dfrac{6G}{R}\,,

(380)

which is an asymptotic solution of
Eq. (
352
) such as Eq. (
374
). Thus,
we see that Type I singularity appears in the model
{F}(R,G)\sim R-\alpha(G/R)
with
\alpha>0
.

•

For the Hubble rate in Eq. (
107
) with
\beta<1
, it is not
possible to express
G
and
R
as the functions of the same
variable (i.e.,
H
or the same combination of
H
and
\dot{H}
).
However, if we examine the asymptotic behavior of
G
and
R
, we have

\displaystyle R\simeq\frac{6h_{s}\beta}{(t_{s}-t)^{\beta+1}}\,,\quad G\simeq\frac{24h_{s}^{3}\beta}{(t_{s}-t)^{3\beta+1}}\,,

(381)

and

\displaystyle\frac{G}{R}\sim(t_{s}-t)^{-2\beta}\sim G^{\frac{2\beta}{3\beta+1}}

(382)

If we replace
G
by
G/R
(as given in Eq. (
382
)) in
Eq. (
331
), then we find that the asymptotic time
dependence in Eq. (
352
) for
\beta<1
is
exactly same as follows:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2\dot{H}+3(1+w)H^{2}\right]\sim\frac{\alpha}{(t_{s}-t)^{\beta+1}}+\frac{\gamma}{(t_{s}-t)^{2\beta}}\,.

(383)

With this consideration, one can derive
a specific
{F}(R,G)
-gravity model (by setting some parameters)
from Eq. (
331
) as

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)=R+\frac{3(3\beta+1)}{2(\beta+1)}\frac{G}{R}\,,

(384)

which may encounter with other types of singularities.
So, in the model
{F}(R,G)\sim R+\bar{\alpha}(G/R)
with
\bar{\alpha}>0
,
Type II, Type III and Type IV singularities may appear.
Then, by substituting
G
for
R
we get

\displaystyle{F}(R,G)\simeq R+\bar{\delta}|R|^{\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}\,,

(385)

which is a well-know result in cosmology. Now we have the following observations:

–

In the model
{F}(R\rightarrow\infty)\sim R+\bar{\delta}R^{\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}
,
for

0<\beta<1
and
\bar{\delta}>0
,
a Type III singularity may appear.

–

In the model
{F}(R\rightarrow-\infty)\sim R+\bar{\delta}|R|^{\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}
,
for

-1<\beta<0

and
\bar{\delta}>0
,
a Type II singularity may appear.

–

In the model
{F}(R\rightarrow 0^{-})\sim R+\bar{\delta}|R|^{\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}
,
for

\beta<-1
and
\beta\not=-n

(being
n
a natural number)
and
\bar{\delta}<0
, a Type IV singularity may appear.

IV.8
F(T)
Gravity

One of the alternative gravitational theories beyond Einstein’s GR
is the Teleparallel Equivalent of GR (TEGR) where instead of the
curvature scalar
R
defined by the Levi-Civita connection one
uses the concept of torsion scalar
T
defined by the
Weitzenböck connection
Hehl:1976kj
. The idea of this
gravitational theory was originally introduced by Einstein in 1928
under the name “Fern-Parallelismus” or “distant parallelism”
or “teleparallelism”
Einstein
;
2005physics…3046U
. It
was found that the modified teleparallel gravity theory
F(T)
in
which
F
is an arbitrary function of the torsion scalar
T
, can
explain the late-time accelerating expansion of the Universe for
some suitable choices of
F(T)

Bengochea:2008gz
;
Linder:2010py
. Moreover, it was also
recognized that the
F(T)
models can also take an active role to
describe the early inflationary phase

Rezazadeh:2017edd
;
Awad:2017ign
;
Bamba:2016wjm
;
Rezazadeh:2015dza
;
Nashed:2014lva
.
With such appealing consequences, the modified teleparallel
gravitation models got massive attention in the astrophysical and
cosmological community

Paliathanasis:2021uvd
;
Paliathanasis:2017htk
;
Paliathanasis:2016vsw
;
Bamba:2016gbu
;
Haro:2014xtk
;
Paliathanasis:2014iva
;
Amoros:2013nxa
;
Basilakos:2013rua
;
Bamba:2012vg
.
We refer to two recent reviews on
F(T)
teleparallel gravity
models
Cai:2015emx
;
Bahamonde:2021gfp
.
In Ref.
Izumi:2012qj
;
Ong:2013qja
, however, it has been proved that superluminal modes appear in the
F(T)
gravity and therefore the
F(T)
gravity model is physically inconsistent.
In this section, however, just for the illustration, we show the structure of the singularities in the
framework of the
F(T)
gravity.

In order to discuss the teleparallelism, we use the orthonormal
tetrad components
e_{A}(x^{\mu})
. We assume that the index
A

runs over
0
,
1
,
2
,
3
for the tangent space at each point

x^{\mu}
of the manifold and
e_{A}^{\mu}
forms the tangent vector
of the manifold. The relation between the metric
g^{\mu\nu}
and
the tetrad components is given by
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{AB}e^{A}_{\mu}\mathrm{e}^{B}_{\nu}
where
\eta_{AB}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
. Compared to
the General theory of Relativity where the torsionless Levi-Civita
connection is used, in Teleparallelism the curvatureless
Weitzenböck connection is used
Weitzenbock
, hence, the
gravitational field is described by the non-null torsion tensor

\displaystyle T^{\rho}_{\ \mu\nu}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\rho}_{A}\left(\partial_{\mu}e^{A}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}e^{A}_{\mu}\right)\,,

(386)

The torsion scalar
T
, one of the main ingredients of the teleparallel gravity, is defined
by
Hayashi:1979qx
;
Maluf:2013gaa
:

\displaystyle T\equiv S_{\rho}^{\ \mu\nu}T^{\rho}_{\ \mu\nu}\,,

(387)

where

\displaystyle S_{\rho}^{\ \mu\nu}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(K^{\mu\nu}_{\ \ \rho}+\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}T^{\alpha\nu}_{\ \ \alpha}-\delta^{\nu}_{\rho}T^{\alpha\mu}_{\ \ \alpha}\right)\,,

(388)

and
K^{\mu\nu}_{\verb||\rho}
, the contorsion tensor is given by

\displaystyle K^{\mu\nu}_{\ \ \rho}\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\left(T^{\mu\nu}_{\ \ \rho}-T^{\nu\mu}_{\ \ \rho}-T_{\rho}^{\ \ \mu\nu}\right)\,.

(389)

The action of the modified teleparallel gravity as

Linder:2010py

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x|e|\left[\frac{F(T)}{2{\kappa}^{2}}+{{L}}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\,,

(390)

|e|=\det\left(e^{A}_{\mu}\right)=\sqrt{-g}
.
Now, varying the action in Eq. (
390
) with
respect to the vierbein vector field
e_{A}^{\mu}
one can obtain the
gravitational field equations
Bengochea:2008gz

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e}\partial_{\mu}\left(eS_{A}^{\ \mu\nu}\right)F^{\prime}-e_{A}^{\lambda}T^{\rho}_{\ \mu\lambda}S_{\rho}^{\ \nu\mu}F^{\prime}+S_{A}^{\ \mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}TF^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{4}e_{A}^{\nu}F=\frac{{\kappa}^{2}}{2}e_{A}^{\rho}{T^{(\mathrm{M})}}_{\rho}^{\ \nu}\,,

(391)

where prime denotes the differentiation with respect to the torsion scalar
T
and
{T^{(\mathrm{M})}}_{\rho}^{\ \nu}
is the energy-momentum tensor describing the entire matter sector.

Now, let us assume that the background manifold is described well
by the spatially flat FLRW Universe (
3
).
Then, vierbein takes the form
e^{A}_{\mu}=\left(1,a(t),a(t),a(t)\right)
where
a(t)
is the
scale factor of the FLRW Universe. One can quickly derive the line
element of the FLRW Universe (
3
).
For this tetrad, using Eqs. (
386
), (
388
), and (
389
), one
can derive the torsion scalar
T=-6H^{2}
.
In this flat FLRW background, one can write down the gravitational equations

\displaystyle H^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\kappa^{2}}{3}(\rho+\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}})=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{3}\rho_{\mathrm{eff}},

(392)

\displaystyle\dot{H}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}(\rho+p+\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}+p_{\mathrm{GDE}})=-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}(\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}+p_{\mathrm{eff}})\,,

(393)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are the
total energy density and total pressure of the Universe, respectively,
\rho

and
p
denote the energy density and pressure of all the perfect
fluids comprising the entire matter sector where they satisfy the
conservation law
\dot{\rho}+3H(p+\rho)=0
. Further,

\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}
and
p_{\mathrm{GDE}}
are given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}=\frac{1}{2{\kappa}^{2}}\left(-T-F+2TF^{\prime}\right)\,,\quad p_{\mathrm{GDE}}=-\frac{1}{2{\kappa}^{2}}\left[\left(4-4F^{\prime}-8TF^{\prime\prime}\right)\dot{H}-T-F+2TF^{\prime}\right]\,.

(394)

Notice that the effective fluid characterized by
\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}
and
p_{\mathrm{GDE}}
satisfies
the continuity equation
\dot{\rho}_{\mathrm{GDE}}+3H(p_{\mathrm{GDE}}+\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}})=0
.
As one can clearly notice that the choice of
F(T)
highly
influences the gravitational equations. In the following we shall
focus on the finite-time future singularities that may appear in
the context of modified teleparallel gravity.

We consider, as in the previous sections, the following Hubble rate in (
61
) and (
209
)
Bamba:2012vg

\displaystyle H\sim

\displaystyle\,\frac{h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{\beta}}\,,\quad\mathrm{for}\,\,\,\beta>0\,,

(395)

\displaystyle H\sim

\displaystyle\,H_{\mathrm{s}}+\frac{h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{\beta}}\,,\quad\mathrm{for}\ \beta<-1\,,\ \mbox{and}\ -1<\beta<0\,.

(396)

One can clearly notice that as
t\rightarrow t_{s}
, finite-time
singularities may appear depending on the nature of
\beta
.
This can be illustrated as follows: when
t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
, for

\beta>0
, both
H\sim h_{\mathrm{s}}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\beta}
and

\dot{H}\sim\beta h_{\mathrm{s}}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta+1\right)}
diverge
to infinity; for
-1<\beta<0
,
H
is finite, but
\dot{H}

becomes infinity; for
\beta<-1
, but
\beta
is not any
integer, both
H
and
\dot{H}
are finite, but the higher
derivatives of
H
can diverge to infinity.

From Eq. (
395
) one can derive the evolution of the
scale factor as

\displaystyle a\sim

\displaystyle\,a_{\mathrm{s}}\exp\left[\frac{h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\beta-1}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}\right]\,,\quad\mathrm{for}\,\,\,0<\beta<1,\;\mbox{and}\,\,\,\beta>1\,,

(397)

\displaystyle a\sim

\displaystyle\,a_{\mathrm{c}}\left(\frac{t_{\mathrm{s}}-t_{\mathrm{c}}}{t_{\mathrm{s}}-t}\right)^{h_{\mathrm{s}}},\quad\mathrm{for}\,\,\,\beta=1\,,

(398)

where
a_{\mathrm{s}}
and
a_{\mathrm{c}}
are some positive constants. Now, from Eq. (
397
) one can see that,
when
t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
,
for
\beta\geq 1
,

a\to\infty
, whilst for
\beta<0
and
0<\beta<1
,

a\to a_{\mathrm{s}}
.
Additionally, one can further notice that from
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=3H^{2}/\kappa^{2}
(Eq. (
392
)) and (
395
) that,
for
\beta>0
,

H\to\infty
and as a result,

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=3H^{2}/\kappa^{2}\to\infty
,
while on the other hand,
for
\beta<0
,
H
asymptotically becomes finite
and
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
asymptotically approaches to a finite
constant value
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}
.
Furthermore, from

\dot{H}\sim\beta h_{\mathrm{s}}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta+1\right)}
and Eq. (
396
) one can see that
for
\beta>-1
,
\dot{H}\to\infty
and as a consequence,

P_{\mathrm{eff}}=-\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)/\kappa^{2}\to\infty
.
While for
\beta<-1
, but
\beta
is not any integer,

a
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
P_{\mathrm{eff}}
are finite since both
H
and
\dot{H}
are finite, however, the higher derivatives of

H
diverges.

Thus, we see that for the Hubble parameter described in
Eqs. (
395
) and (
396
) the
F(T)

gravity models can encounter with the finite-time singularities
for the following values of
\beta
:

•

For
\beta\geq 1
, Type I (“Big Rip”) singularity appears.

•

For
-1<\beta<0
, Type II (“sudden”) singularity appears.

•

For
0<\beta<1
, Type III singularity appears.

•

For
\beta<-1
, but
\beta
is not any integer,
Type IV singularity appears.

future singularities for different values of
\beta
in the
expressions of the Hubble parameter given in
Eqs. (
395
) and (
396
) along with the behavior of
H
and
\dot{H}
in the limit of
t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
. Table courtesy
Ref.
Bamba:2012vg
.

of

In TABLE
4
, we summarize the
appearance of finite-time singularities out of the expressions of
the Hubble parameter as given in Eqs. (
395
) and
(
396
). Thus, one can see that the above expressions
of the Hubble parameter can be used to reconstruct the
F(T)

gravity models which may allow the finite-time singularities. We
focus on the finite-time singularities when the geometrical sector
dominates over the matter sector, that means when
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\simeq\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}\simeq p_{\mathrm{GDE}}
, that
means
w_{\mathrm{eff}}=p_{\mathrm{eff}}/\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\simeq p_{\rm GDE}/\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}
. Following this, one can have

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{eff}}\simeq w_{\mathrm{GDE}}=-\frac{\left(4-4F^{\prime}-8TF^{\prime\prime}\right)\dot{H}-T-F+2TF^{\prime}}{-T-F+2TF^{\prime}}\,.

(399)

On the other hand, from Eq. (
394
) one can write

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{GDE}}=-\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}+I(H,\dot{H})\,,

(400)

where
I(H,\dot{H})=-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2-2F^{\prime}-4TF^{\prime\prime}\right]\dot{H}
.
From Eq.(
393
)
one can quickly write
p_{\mathrm{GDE}}=-\rho_{\mathrm{GDE}}-2\dot{H}/\kappa^{2}
, where we have using that the geometrical sector dominates over the matter one. Now, comparing this with (
400
),
we obtain the following differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{H}+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}I(H,\dot{H})=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\dot{H}\left[F^{\prime}+2TF^{\prime\prime}\right]=0\,.

(401)

In the following we shall illustrate the the appearance of the finite-time singularities in a specific
F(T)
gravity model having the power law form:

\displaystyle F(T)=AT^{\alpha}\,,

(402)

where
A~(\neq 0)
and
\alpha~(\neq 0)
are constants. With this
choice of
F(T)
, Eq. (
401
) becomes

\displaystyle\left(2\alpha-1\right)H^{2\left(\alpha-1\right)}=0\,.

(403)

Notice that for both Eqs. (
395
) and
(
396
),
\dot{H}\neq 0
. In the limit of
t\rightarrow t_{s}
, Eq. (
403
) needs to be
satisfied. Now, from Eqs. (
395
) and
(
396
), one can see that for
\beta>0
[here two different cases may arise in this way: (i)
\beta\geq 1

(i.e., Type I singularity) and
0<\beta<1
(Type III
singularity)],
\alpha<1
, so that
Eq. (
403
) is asymptotically satisfied. While on
the other hand, for
\beta<0
[here one can similarly explore two
different cases:
-1<\beta<0
(Type II singularity and
\beta<-1

(Type IV singularity)],
\alpha=1/2
, in which
Eq. (
403
) is always satisfied. At this point it
is very crucial to mention that the conditions: (i) for
\beta>0
(Type I singularity corresponds to
\beta\geq 1
and Type III
singularity corresponds to
0<\beta<1
),
\alpha<0
, while (ii)
for
\beta<0
(Type II singularity corresponds to
-1<\beta<0

and Type IV singularity corresponds to
\beta<-1
),
\alpha=1/2

are “necessary conditions” for the appearance of the finite-time
future singularities but they are not the sufficient conditions.
Because if
\alpha<0
, then the Type I singularity with
\beta\geq 1
appears rather than the Type III singularity with

0<\beta<1
, since in the limit of
t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
, both
H

and
\dot{H}
with
\beta\geq 1
diverge more rapidly than those
with
0<\beta<1
. This is because of the absolute value of the power
\beta
, namely, the absolute value of
\beta
for the Type
I singularity (
\beta\geq 1
) is larger than the absolute value
of
\beta
for the Type III singularity (
0<\beta<1
). As a
consequence, the Type I singularity is realized faster than the
Type III singularity, and this results in the appearance of the
Type I singularity. In a similar fashion, if
\alpha=1/2
, then
Type IV singularity with
\beta<-1
appears compared to the Type
II singularity with
-1<\beta<0
because again in the limit of
t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
,
H\to H_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\dot{H}\to 0

with
\beta<-1
are realized more quickly than
H\to H_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\dot{H}\to\infty
with
-1<\beta<0
. This
is again because of the absolute value of the power
\beta
,
namely, the absolute value of
\beta
for the Type IV singularity
(
\beta<-1
) is larger than the absolute value of
\beta
for the
Type II singularity (
-1<\beta<0
). Hence, the Type IV singularity
appears faster than the Type II singularity, and Type IV
singularity appears as a result.

Finally, we note that Type V (“
w
”) singularity can also appear in this model. In the Type V singularity,
a specific choice of the scale factor can be considered
Dabrowski:2009kg

\displaystyle a(t)=

\displaystyle\,a_{\mathrm{s}}\left(1-\frac{3\sigma}{2}\left\{\frac{n-1}{n-\left[2/\left(3\sigma\right)\right]}\right\}^{n-1}\right)^{-1}+\frac{1-2/\left(3\sigma\right)}{n-2/\left(3\sigma\right)}\times na_{\mathrm{s}}\left(1-\frac{2}{3\sigma}\left\{\frac{n-\left[2/\left(3\sigma\right)\right]}{n-1}\right\}^{n-1}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{s}}}\right)^{2/\left(3\sigma\right)}

\displaystyle\,+a_{\mathrm{s}}\left(\frac{3\sigma}{2}\left\{\frac{n-1}{n-\left[2/\left(3\sigma\right)\right]}\right\}^{n-1}-1\right)^{-1}\left[1-\frac{1-2/\left(3\sigma\right)}{n-2/\left(3\sigma\right)}\frac{t}{t_{\mathrm{s}}}\right]^{n}\,,

(404)

where
n

\sigma
are any arbitrary real numbers. In the limit of

t\to t_{\mathrm{s}}
, we have
H(t\to t_{\mathrm{s}})\to 0

and
\dot{H}(t\to t_{\mathrm{s}})\to 0
. The effective EoS

w_{\mathrm{eff}}=\left(1/3\right)\left(2q-1\right)\to\infty
where
q\equiv-\ddot{a}a/\dot{a}^{2}
is the deceleration
parameter of the Universe. Thus, for the power law
model (
402
) with
\alpha>1
,
Eq. (
403
) can be satisfied asymptotically.
Hence, the Type V (“
w
”) singularity can appear in this model.

IV.9
Non-local Gravity

Along with the known modified gravitational theories presented in
the earlier sections, an addition to the class of modified gravity
theories is the non-local gravitational theory, i.e., non-local
additions to GR
Deser:2007jk
. These non-local corrections
naturally arise due to quantum effects and with such new
corrections, it is also possible to explain the accelerating
expansion of the Universe
Deser:2007jk
. In this section we
shall describe how the finite-time future singularities may appear in this gravitational theory.

The action of the non-local gravity is given by
Bamba:2012ky

\displaystyle S=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\left\{R\left(1+f(\Box^{-1}R)\right)-2\Lambda\right\}+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\left(Q;g\right)\Bigg]\,,

(405)

where
R
is the Ricci scalar,
g
is the determinant of the metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
,
\Box\equiv g^{\mu\nu}{\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}

with
{\nabla}_{\mu}
being the covariant derivative
is the covariant d’Alembertian for a scalar field, and
f
is any arbitrary function of
\Box^{-1}R
,
\Lambda
is a cosmological constant,
and
{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\left(Q;g\right)
denotes the matter Lagrangian in which

Q
stands for the matter fields.

Introducing two scalar fields
\eta

and
\xi
, we can rewrite the above action (
405
) as

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\,\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\Bigg\{R\left(1+f(\eta)\right)+\xi\left(\Box\eta-R\right)-2\Lambda\Bigg\}+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\Bigg\{R\left(1+f(\eta)\right)-\partial_{\mu}\xi\partial^{\mu}\eta-\xi R-2\Lambda\Bigg\}+{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\Bigg]\,.

(406)

In the context of a spatially flat
FLRW metric (
3
), one can write down the
gravitational field equations as

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,-3H^{2}\left(1+f(\eta)-\xi\right)+\frac{1}{2}\dot{\xi}\dot{\eta}-3H\left(f^{\prime}(\eta)\dot{\eta}-\dot{\xi}\right)+\Lambda+\kappa^{2}\rho\,,

(407)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)\left(1+f(\eta)-\xi\right)+\frac{1}{2}\dot{\xi}\dot{\eta}+\left(\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}+2H\frac{d}{dt}\right)\left(f(\eta)-\xi\right)-\Lambda+\kappa^{2}p\,,

(408)

where we have considered that the scalar fields
\eta
and
\xi
depend only on time
Bamba:2012ky
. Note that here
\rho
and
p
are respectively the energy density and pressure of the matter sector.
One can further write down
the equations of motion for the scalar fields
\eta
and
\xi
as

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\ddot{\eta}+3H\dot{\eta}+6\dot{H}+12H^{2}\,,

(409)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,\ddot{\xi}+3H\dot{\xi}-\left(6\dot{H}+12H^{2}\right)f^{\prime}(\eta)\,,

(410)

where we have used
R=6\dot{H}+12H^{2}
. Let us now focus on
the emergence of the finite-time singularities in this
gravitational theory.

IV.9.1
Finite-time Singularities

We start with the following expression of the
Hubble parameter in (
61
) or (
107
) (see
Bamba:2012ky
).
The scale factor is also given in (
61
).
Now with the use of
\ddot{\eta}+3H\dot{\eta}=a^{-3}d\left(a^{3}\dot{\eta}\right)/dt
and
Eq. (
409
), one can express
\eta
as

\displaystyle\eta=-\int^{t}\frac{1}{a^{3}}\left(\int^{\bar{t}}Ra^{3}d\bar{t}\right)dt\,.

(411)

We note that in the limit
t\to t_{s}
: for
\beta>1
,
\dot{H}\ll H^{2}
, and hence,
R\sim 12H^{2}
,
whilst for
-1<\beta<0
and
0<\beta<1
,
\dot{H}\gg H^{2}
, and hence,
R\sim 6\dot{H}
. By
applying these relations to Eq. (
411
) and then taking
the leading term in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
,
one can express
\eta
for different regions of
\beta
as follows

Bamba:2012ky
:

\displaystyle\eta\sim

\displaystyle\,-\frac{4h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\beta-1}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}+{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\quad(\beta>1)\,,

(412)

\displaystyle\eta\sim

\displaystyle\,-\frac{6h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\beta-1}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}+{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\quad(-1<\beta<0\,,\,0<\beta<1)\,,

(413)

where
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}
is an integration constant.
As discussed in
Bamba:2012ky
, for

{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}=0
, the finite-time future singularities depicted by
the Hubble parameter in Eq. (
61
) or (
107
) do not appear, however, for
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq 0
, finite time future singularities can occur.

For
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq 0
, note that, if the power in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
is negative, i.e.
-\left(\beta-1\right)<0
, then the first term proportional to
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}
is the leading one. On the other hand, if the power in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
is positive, i.e.
-\left(\beta-1\right)>0
, then the second constant term becomes the leading one. Thus,
for
\beta>1
, the first term
is the leading one, i.e.,

\eta\propto\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}
, while for

-1<\beta<0
and
0<\beta<1
, the second term
is the leading one, i.e.,
\eta\sim{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}
.
For
\beta=1
in Eq. (
61
) or (
107
), it follows from Eq. (
409
) that,

\displaystyle\eta\sim 6h_{\mathrm{s}}\left[\left(1+2h_{\mathrm{s}}\right)/\left(1+3h_{\mathrm{s}}\right)\right]\ln\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)+{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}.

(414)

We continue by taking a particular form of
f(\eta)
as
Bamba:2012ky

f(\eta)=f_{\mathrm{s}}\eta^{\sigma}\,,

(415)

where
f_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\sigma
are non-zero constants.
Now, using

\ddot{\xi}+3H\dot{\xi}=a^{-3}d\left(a^{3}\dot{\xi}\right)/dt

and Eq. (
410
),

\xi
can be expressed as

\xi=\int^{t}\frac{1}{a^{3}}\left(\int^{\bar{t}}\frac{df(\eta)}{d\eta}Ra^{3}d\bar{t}\right)dt\,.

(416)

Now we apply the conditions
R\sim 12H^{2}
(for
\beta>1
) and
R\sim 6\dot{H}
(for
\beta<1
)
to Eq. (
416
) and
taking into account the leading term in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
, we obtain
Bamba:2012ky

\displaystyle\xi\sim-f_{\mathrm{s}}\left(-\frac{4h_{\mathrm{s}}}{\beta-1}\right)^{\sigma}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma}+{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}~,\quad(\beta>1)\,,

(417)

\displaystyle\xi\sim\frac{6f_{\mathrm{s}}h_{\mathrm{s}}\sigma{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\sigma-1}}{\beta-1}\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}+{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}~,\quad(-1<\beta<0\,~,\,0<\beta<1)\,,

(418)

where
{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}
is the constant of integration.
Now, if the power in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
is negative, i.e.
-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma<0
, the first term proportional to

\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma}
is the leading one. On the other hand, if the power in terms of
\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)
is
positive, i.e.
-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma>0
, then the second constant term becomes the leading one.
Therefore, we see that for
\beta>1
,
\sigma>0
,

\xi\propto\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma}
, while
for (
\beta>1
,
\sigma<0
) and
(
-1<\beta<0\,,\,0<\beta<1
),

\xi\sim{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}
. Therefore one arrives at the following three cases
Bamba:2012ky
:

•

For
\beta>1
,
\sigma>0
:
\eta\propto\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}

and

\xi\propto\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)\sigma}
.

•

For
\beta>1
,
\sigma<0
:
\eta\propto\left(t_{\mathrm{s}}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}

and

\xi\sim{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

•

For
-1<\beta<0\,,\,0<\beta<1
:
\eta\sim{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\xi\sim{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

Now, having all of these, following
Bamba:2012ky
one can further investigate the possibility of the finite time future singularities in this gravitational theory when the Hubble rate is given in Eq. (
61
) or (
107
) and the results are as follows

•

For
\sigma<0
, with
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq 0
and
{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}=1
, Type I (Big Rip) singularity can occur for
\beta>1
.

•

If
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq 0
, and

f_{\mathrm{s}}{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\sigma-1}\left(6\sigma-{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)+{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}-1=0
,
then Type II (sudden) singularity can occur for
-1<\beta<0
.

•

If
{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\neq 0
, and

f_{\mathrm{s}}{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\sigma-1}\left(6\sigma-{\eta}_{\mathrm{c}}\right)+{\xi}_{\mathrm{c}}-1=0
,
there Type III singularity can occur for
0<\beta<1
.

IV.10
Non-minimal Maxwell-Einstein Gravity

In this section, we discuss the finite-time future singularities
appearing in the non-minimal Maxwell-Einstein gravity with general
gravitational coupling. To begin with, we consider the following
action
Bamba:2008ja
;
Bamba:2008ut
:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{GR}}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\left[\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}R-\frac{1}{4}I(R)F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\right]\,,

(419)

where
I(R)
is any function of the Ricci scalar,
F_{\mu\nu}={\partial}_{\mu}A_{\nu}-{\partial}_{\nu}A_{\mu}

is the electromagnetic field-strength tensor in which
A_{\mu}
denotes the
U(1)

gauge field. It is well-known that the coupling between the scalar curvature and
the Lagrangian of the electromagnetic field, as shown in action (
419
), arises in curved
spacetime due to one-loop vacuum-polarization effects in
Quantum Electrodynamics
Drummond:1979pp
.

Now. taking the variations of the action in
Eq. (
419
) with respect to the metric

g_{\mu\nu}
and the
U(1)
gauge field
A_{\mu}
, one can find
the gravitational field equations and the equation of motion of

A_{\mu}
, respectively, as
Bamba:2008ja
;
Bamba:2008ut

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R=\kappa^{2}T^{(\mathrm{EM})}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(420)

and

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}{\partial}_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}I(R)F^{\mu\nu}\right)=0\,,

(421)

where
T^{(\mathrm{EM})}_{\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
420
)
denotes the contribution to the energy-momentum tensor from the
electromagnetic field:

\displaystyle T^{(\mathrm{EM})}_{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\,I(R)\left(g^{\alpha\beta}F_{\mu\beta}F_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{2}\left[I^{\prime}(R)F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}R_{\mu\nu}+g_{\mu\nu}\Box\left(I^{\prime}(R)F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)-{\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}\left(I^{\prime}(R)F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}\right)\right]\,,

(422)

in which the prime refers to the derivative with respect to
R
;

\nabla_{\mu}
denotes the covariant derivative operator
associated with
g_{\mu\nu}
, and
\equiv g_{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}
is the covariant d’Alembertian for a
scalar field. To understand the dynamics of the Universe within
this gravitational theory, we consider the flat FLRW metric and following Ref.

Bamba:2008ut
we consider the case where only the effects of
magnetic fields are present that means the effects of electric
fields are negligible. Additionally, we further consider that only
one component of the magnetic field
\vec{B}
is non-zero, that
means other two components are zero. Thus, it follows from

\mathrm{div}\vec{B}=0
that the off-diagonal components of the
last term of the r.h.s. of Eq. (
422
) for

T^{(\mathrm{EM})}_{\mu\nu}
, i.e.,
{\nabla}_{\mu}{\nabla}_{\nu}\left[I^{\prime}(R)F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}\right]
are zero. Hence, all of the off-diagonal components of

T^{(\mathrm{EM})}_{\mu\nu}
are zero. Furthermore, since we have
assumed that we only have the magnetic fields as background
quantities at the
0^{\mathrm{th}}
order, therefore, the magnetic
fields remain independent of the space components
\vec{x}
.

In the FLRW spacetime, the equation of motion for the

U(1)
gauge field in the Coulomb gauge,
{\partial}^{j}A_{j}(t,\vec{x})=0
,
and the case of
A_{0}(t,\vec{x})=0
, becomes

\displaystyle{\ddot{A}}_{i}(t,\vec{x})+\left(H+\frac{\dot{I}}{I}\right){\dot{A}}(t,\vec{x})-\frac{1}{a^{2}}\mathop{\Delta}\limits^{(3)}\,A_{i}(t,\vec{x})=0\,,

(423)

where
\mathop{\Delta}\limits^{(3)}={\partial}^{i}{\partial}_{i}
is the flat 3-dimensional Laplacian.
From Eq. (
423
) it follows that the Fourier mode
A_{i}(k,t)

satisfies the equation

\displaystyle{\ddot{A}}_{i}(k,t)+\left(H+\frac{\dot{I}}{I}\right){\dot{A}}_{i}(k,t)+\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}A_{i}(k,t)=0\,,

(424)

which can alternatively be expressed (by replacing the cosmic time
t
in terms of the conformal time

\eta
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}A_{i}(k,\eta)}{\partial\eta^{2}}+\frac{1}{I(\eta)}\frac{dI(\eta)}{d\eta}\frac{\partial A_{i}(k,\eta)}{\partial\eta}+k^{2}A_{i}(k,\eta)=0\,.

(425)

We note that finding the exact solution of Eq. (
425
)
for any arbitrary coupling function
I(\eta)
is not possible while
with the use of the Wentzel–Kramers–Brillouin (WKB) approximation on subhorizon scales and the
long-wavelength approximation on superhorizon scales, and finally matching these
solutions at the horizon crossing
Bamba:2006ga
;
Bamba:2007sx
, an
approximate solution can be found as

\displaystyle\left|A_{i}(k,\eta)\right|^{2}=|\bar{C}(k)|^{2}=\frac{1}{2kI(\eta_{k})}\left|1-\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{kI(\eta_{k})}\frac{dI(\eta_{k})}{d\eta}+i\right]k\int_{\eta_{k}}^{{\eta}_{\mathrm{f}}}\frac{I(\eta_{k})}{I\left(\widetilde{\widetilde{\eta}}\right)}d\widetilde{\widetilde{\eta}}\,\right|^{2}\,,

(426)

where
\eta_{k}
and
{\eta}_{\mathrm{f}}
denotes the conformal time
at the horizon-crossing and the conformal time at the end of inflation, respectively.
From Eq. (
426
),one may obtain the amplitude of the proper magnetic fields in the position space

\displaystyle|{B_{i}}^{(\mathrm{proper})}(t)|^{2}=\frac{k|\bar{C}(k)|^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\frac{k^{4}}{a^{4}}\,,

(427)

on a comoving scale
L=2\pi/k
. From Eq. (
427
) one can
see that the proper magnetic fields evolve as

|{B_{i}}^{(\mathrm{proper})}(t)|^{2}=|\bar{B}|^{2}/a^{4}
, where

|\bar{B}|
is a constant
Bamba:2008ut
. This means that the
impact of the coupling function
I
on the value of the proper
magnetic fields exists only during the inflationary stage. On the
other hand, the conductivity of the Universe,

{\sigma}_{\mathrm{c}}
, is extremely small during the period of
inflation since at that time only a few charged particles exist.
After the reheating stage, a number of charged particles are
produced, so that the conductivity immediately jumps to a large
value
{\sigma}_{\mathrm{c}}\gg H
. Consequently, for a large
enough conductivity at the reheating stage, the proper magnetic
fields evolve in proportion to
a^{-2}(t)
in the
radiation-dominated stage and in the subsequent matter-dominated
stage
Ratra:1991bn
.

Now, from (
422
), one can express the effective
energy density and effective pressure of the Universe as follows

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\left\{\frac{I(R)}{2}+3\left[-\left(5H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)I^{\prime}(R)+6H\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)I^{\prime\prime}(R)\right]\right\}\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a^{4}}\,,

(428)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{eff}}=

\displaystyle\,\biggl[-\frac{I(R)}{6}+\left(-H^{2}+5\dot{H}\right)I^{\prime}(R)-6\left(-20H^{2}\dot{H}+4\dot{H}^{2}-H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)I^{\prime\prime}(R)

\displaystyle\,-36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}I^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\biggr]\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a^{4}}\,,

(429)

where the following relations under the assumption of
negligible electric fields have been used:

g^{\alpha\beta}F_{0\beta}F_{0\alpha}-\left(1/4\right)g_{00}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}=|{B_{i}}^{(\mathrm{proper})}(t)|^{2}/2,

and

F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}=2|{B_{i}}^{(\mathrm{proper})}(t)|^{2}
.

We recall that
in terms of the effective energy density and pressure, the Friedmann and Raychaudhuri
equations for this gravitational theory can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}\ =

\displaystyle\,\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\,,

(430)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)=

\displaystyle\,p_{\mathrm{eff}}\,,

(431)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are
given in Eqs. (
428
) and (
429
), respectively.
Since the Friedmann and Raychaudhuri equations (
430
) and (
431
) includes
I(R)
, therefore, depending on the functional forms of
I(R)
,
the Maxwell-Einstein gravity may allow finite-time future singularities. In the following we discuss the possible finite-time future singularities.

(i)
Big Rip singularity

We consider the Hubble rate in (
176
).
For this Hubble rate, one can derive the scale factor and the Ricci scalar as in (
62
) and (
354
).
We assume that for the large curvature,
I(R)
behaves as

\displaystyle I(R)\sim I_{s}R^{\alpha},

(432)

where
I_{s}
and
\alpha
are constants. Thus, with the choice of

I(R)
in Eq. (
432
),
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
in
Eq. (
428
) behaves as
\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-2\alpha+4h_{s}}
while from the l.h.s. of the Friedmann equation, i.e.,
Eq. (
431
)
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
behaves as
\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-2}
. From the consistency in
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
, we have,

\displaystyle-2=-2\alpha+4h_{s},\quad\mbox{i.e., }\quad\alpha=1+2h_{s}\,.

(433)

Now, from Eq. (
431
) one can write,

\displaystyle\frac{3h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}=

\displaystyle\,I_{s}\left(12h_{s}^{2}+6h_{s}\right)^{\alpha-2}

\displaystyle\,\times\left\{\frac{\left(12h_{s}^{2}+6h_{s}\right)^{2}}{2}+3\left[-\alpha\left(12h_{s}^{2}+6h_{s}\right)\left(h_{s}+5h_{s}^{2}\right)+6\alpha\left(\alpha-1\right)h_{s}\left(2h_{s}+4h_{s}^{2}\right)\right]\right\}\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a_{s}^{4}}~,

(434)

which can be simplified with the use of
\alpha
from (
433
) as

\displaystyle\frac{3h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}=-\frac{I_{s}h_{s}\left(12h_{s}^{2}+6h_{s}\right)^{\alpha}|\bar{B}|^{2}}{2a_{s}^{4}}\,,

(435)

which clearly infers that
I_{s}
must be negative.
This tells that
I(R)
is also negative
and therefore the gauge field becomes ghost and the model becomes physically inconsistent.

Thus, it follows from Eqs. (
432
) and
(
433
) that the Big rip singularity as described
through the Hubble rate in Eq. (
176
) may
emerge when for the large curvature,
I(R)
behaves as

R^{1+2h_{s}}
. However, for other choices of
I(R)
, we do not get
the Big rip singularity. We in fact note that
for
I(R)=I_{s}R^{\alpha}
,
H=h_{s}/\left(t_{s}-t\right)
is an exact solution.

(ii)
Other types of singularities

We now focus on other types of singularities that may arise for a more general choice of
I(R)
.
Thus, we consider the general case where the Hubble parameter takes the form of (
176
).
For this choice of the Hubble rate, one can find the scale factor and the scalar curvature as follows

\displaystyle a=a_{s}\exp\left[\frac{h_{s}}{\beta-1}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}\right]\,,\quad R=6h_{s}\left[\beta+2h_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta-1\right)}\right]\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\left(\beta+1\right)}\,,

(436)

where
a_{s}
is a positive constant. Similar to the previous case,
we assume that in the large curvature,
I(R)
behaves as in
Eq. (
432
). We note that the case with
\beta>1

is unphysical because, for
\beta>1
,
a\to\infty
in the limit

t\rightarrow t_{s}
, and as a consequence,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
since

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\propto a^{-4}
and
p_{\mathrm{eff}}\propto a^{-4}
. On the other hand,
H\to\infty
as
t\rightarrow t_{s}
. Thus, we see that for

\beta>1
, Eqs. (
430
) and (
431
) are not satisfied
leading to a unphysical case. Therefore, we restrict ourselves to

\beta<1
and explore the possible singularities as follows.

•

If
0<\beta<1
and
\alpha>0
, then
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
in
Eq. (
430
) evolves as
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\propto\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\alpha\left(\beta+1\right)}
, however, looking
at Eq. (
431
), we see that
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
evolves as

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\propto\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-2\beta}
.
Therefore, from the consistency, we must have,

\displaystyle-2\beta=-\alpha\left(\beta+1\right)\ \Longrightarrow\ \beta=\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha},\quad\mbox{or}\quad\alpha=\frac{2\beta}{\beta+1}\,.

(437)

Now, from Eq. (
431
), one can find,

\displaystyle\frac{3h_{s}^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}=-\frac{I_{s}\left(6h_{s}\beta\right)^{\alpha}\left(1-\beta\right)|\bar{B}|^{2}}{2a_{s}^{4}\left(\beta+1\right)}\,,

(438)

where we have used Eq. (
437
). Notice that
Eq. (
438
) demands
I_{s}
to be negative. Thus, in this
case (i.e.,
\alpha>0
and
0<\beta<1
), we see that, in the
limit
t\to t_{s}
,
a\to a_{s}
,
R\to\infty
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\infty
, and
|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to\infty
. Hence, the Type
III singularity appears.

•

If
-1<\beta<0
and
\left(\beta-1\right)/\left(\beta+1\right)<\alpha<0
, then in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
a\to a_{s}
,
R\to\infty
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
, and

|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to\infty
. Although we can see that the final
value of
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
is not a finite value but it vanishes
(
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow t_{s}
),
however, this singularity can be considered to be the singularity
of Type II. Because, when
I
and
H
are given by
I=1+I_{s}R^{\alpha}
and
H=H_{s}+h_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{-\beta}

(where
H_{s}
is a constant), respectively, then in the limit
t\rightarrow t_{s}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\rho_{s}
, a constant, where

\rho_{s}
can be found from Eqs. (
428
) and
(
430
) as
\rho_{s}=3H_{s}^{2}/\kappa^{2}=|\bar{B}|^{2}/\left(2_{s}^{4}\right)
.

•

If
\beta<-1
, then
in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
a\to a_{s}
and
R\to 0
. Now,
for
\alpha\geq\left(\beta-1\right)/\left(\beta+1\right)
,
in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
,

|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to 0
, and higher derivatives of
H

diverge. That means, Type IV singularity appears.

We further note here that for
-1<\beta<0
and
\alpha>0
,

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\infty
, but
H\to 0
in the limit
t\rightarrow t_{s}
, which shows that Eq. (
430
) is not satisfied.
That means, the case with
\alpha>0
and
-1<\beta<0
is not physical in the sense that the Friedmann equation
is inconsistent.
We also find that If
\alpha<0
and
0<\beta<1
, then
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
, but
H\to\infty
.
Hence, Eq. (
430
) is again not satisfied.
Further, if
-1<\beta<0
and
\alpha\leq\left(\beta-1\right)/\left(\beta+1\right)
,
then we have unphysical scenario because in this case, in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
a\to a_{s}
,
R\to\infty
,

\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
, and
|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to 0
, but

\dot{H}\to\infty
. Thus, we see that Eq. (
431
) is not
satisfied.

We mention that if
I(R)
is a constant
6
6

6

Let us note
that the case
I(R)=1
corresponds to the ordinary Maxwell theory.
, we do not find any finite-time future singularity.
We close this section with two examples of
I(R)
as follows. Suppose

I(R)
takes the Hu–Sawicki
form given by
Hu:2007nk

\displaystyle I(R)=I_{\mathrm{HS}}(R)\equiv\frac{c_{1}\left(R/m^{2}\right)^{n}}{c_{2}\left(R/m^{2}\right)^{n}+1}\,,

(439)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are dimensionless constants,
n
is a
positive constant, and
m
denotes a mass scale. We see that the
model in Eq. (
439
) satisfies the following conditions: (i)

\lim_{R\to\infty}I_{\mathrm{HS}}(R)=c_{1}/c_{2}=\mbox{const}
and (ii)
\lim_{R\to 0}I_{\mathrm{HS}}(R)=0
.
The second example of
I(R)
having the same features as with the
Hu–Sawicki form of
I(R)
in Eq. (
439
) takes the
form
Nojiri:2007as

\displaystyle I(R)=I_{\mathrm{NO}}(R)\equiv\frac{\left[\left(R/M^{2}\right)-\left(R_{\mathrm{c}}/M^{2}\right)\right]^{2q+1}+{\left(R_{\mathrm{c}}/M^{2}\right)}^{2q+1}}{c_{3}+c_{4}\left\{\left[\left(R/M^{2}\right)-\left(R_{\mathrm{c}}/M^{2}\right)\right]^{2q+1}+{\left(R_{\mathrm{c}}/M^{2}\right)}^{2q+1}\right\}}\,,

(440)

where
c_{3}
and
c_{4}
are dimensionless constants,
q
is a
positive integer,
M
denotes a mass scale, and
R_{\mathrm{c}}
is
current curvature. One can check that
I(R)
given in
Eq. (
440
) satisfies the conditions: (i)

\lim_{R\to\infty}I_{\mathrm{NO}}(R)=1/c_{4}=\mbox{const}
and
(ii)
\lim_{R\to 0}I_{\mathrm{NO}}(R)=0.
If
\beta<-1
and

I(R)
is given either by
I_{\mathrm{HS}}(R)
in
Eq. (
439
) or
I_{\mathrm{NO}}(R)
in
Eq. (
440
), in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
a\to a_{s}
,
R\to 0
,
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}\to 0
, and
|p_{\mathrm{eff}}|\to 0
.
In addition, higher derivatives of
H
diverge. Thus, the Type IV
singularity emerges. Thus, we see that the Maxwell theory which is
non-minimally coupled to Einstein gravity may produce finite-time
future singularities depending on the form of non-minimal
gravitational coupling.

We conclude that the general conditions on
I(R)
for which the finite-time future
singularities characterized by Eq. (
176
)
cannot emerge are that in the limit
t\to t_{s}
,
I(R)\to\bar{I}

(where
\bar{I}(\neq 0)
is a finite constant),

I^{\prime}(R)\to 0
,
I^{\prime\prime}(R)\to 0
, and

I^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\to 0
.

J.1:
Influence of non-minimal gravitational coupling on the
finite-time future singularities in modified gravity

In this section, we discuss the influence of non-minimal
gravitational coupling of the electromagnetic field on the
F(R)

gravity theory
Bamba:2008ut
. This is a generalization of
the previous section
IV.10
. In this
case, the total energy density and pressure of the Universe are contributed as,

\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}=\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}+\rho_{\mathrm{MG}}
and

p_{\mathrm{tot}}=p_{\mathrm{eff}}+p_{\mathrm{MG}}
, respectively, where
\rho_{\mathrm{eff}}
and

p_{\mathrm{eff}}
are given by Eqs. (
428
) and
(
429
), respectively. And
\rho_{\mathrm{MG}}
and

p_{\mathrm{MG}}
can be found from Eqs. (
167
) and
(
168
) as follows

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{MG}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}f(R)+3\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)f^{\prime}(R)-18\left(4H^{2}\dot{H}+H\ddot{H}\right)f^{\prime\prime}(R)\right]\,,

(441)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{MG}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}f(R)-\left(3H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)f^{\prime}(R)+6\left(8H^{2}\dot{H}+4{\dot{H}}^{2}+6H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)f^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}f^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\right]\,.

(442)

In this case, it follows from Eqs. (
104
), (
428
), (
429
),
(
441
) and (
442
) that the flat Friedmann
and Raychaudhuri equations can be given by
Bamba:2008ut

\displaystyle\frac{3}{\kappa^{2}}H^{2}=\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}=

\displaystyle\,\left\{\frac{I(R)}{2}+3\left[-\left(5H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)I^{\prime}(R)+6H\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)I^{\prime\prime}(R)\right]\right\}\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a^{4}}

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(F(R)-R\right)+3\left(H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)\left(F^{\prime}(R)-1\right)-18\left(4H^{2}\dot{H}+H\ddot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(R)\right]\,,

(443)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left(2\dot{H}+3H^{2}\right)=p_{\mathrm{tot}}=

\displaystyle\,\biggl[-\frac{I(R)}{6}+\left(-H^{2}+5\dot{H}\right)I^{\prime}(R)-6\left(-20H^{2}\dot{H}+4\dot{H}^{2}-H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)I^{\prime\prime}(R)

\displaystyle\,-36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}I^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\biggr]\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a^{4}}+\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\left(F(R)-R\right)-\left(3H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)\left(F^{\prime}(R)-1\right)

\displaystyle\,+6\left(8H^{2}\dot{H}+4{\dot{H}}^{2}+6H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\biggr]\,.

(444)

Now, using Eqs. (
443
) and (
444
), we find

\displaystyle\Bigg[\frac{I(R)}{3}+2\left(-8H^{2}+\dot{H}\right)I^{\prime}(R)+6\left(32H^{2}\dot{H}-4\dot{H}^{2}+4H\ddot{H}-\dddot{H}\right)I^{\prime\prime}(R)-36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}I^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\Bigg]\frac{|\bar{B}|^{2}}{a^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\left[2\dot{H}F^{\prime}(R)+6\left(-4H^{2}\dot{H}+4\dot{H}^{2}+3H\ddot{H}+\dddot{H}\right)F^{\prime\prime}(R)+36\left(4H\dot{H}+\ddot{H}\right)^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)\right]=0\,.

(445)

Recalling that in modified gravity with the ordinary Maxwell
theory, if
F(R)
behaves as in Eq. (
207
), i.e.,

\displaystyle F(R)\sim F_{0}R+F_{1}R^{q}\,,\quad\mbox{where $q$ is a constant}\,,

(446)

a finite-time future singularity appears.
Thus, one may investigate the possibility of finite-time singularities when the
non-minimal gravitational electromagnetic theory is taken into
account in this
F(R)
-gravity model.

Now, when by putting
\beta=-u
in (
107
),
H
behaves as
Bamba:2008ut

\displaystyle H\sim h_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{u}\,,

(447)

where
u
is an positive integer, then there exists no finite-time
future singularity. In what follows, we consider the case when
u\geq 2
.
From Eq. (
447
), one finds that

\displaystyle R\sim 6\dot{H}\sim-6uh_{s}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{u-1}\,,\quad a\sim a_{s}\exp\left[-\frac{h_{s}}{u+1}\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{u+1}\right]\,,

(448)

where in the expression of
R
we have considered only the leading term.

Now, we examine the non-minimal gravitational coupling of the
electromagnetic field characterized by
I(R)
which gives the
solution in Eq. (
448
). We again consider that
I(R)
behaves as in Eq. (
432
).
Concerning Eq. (
445
), its first and second terms in the left
hand side are coming from the non-minimal
gravitational electromagnetic coupling and the modified-gravity
sectors, respectively. When
t
is close to
t_{s}
, the leading term of the
non-minimal gravitational electromagnetic coupling sector evolves
as
\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{\left(u-1\right)\left(\alpha-1\right)-2}
.
On the other hand, if
q\leq 1
, or
q>1
and
u<q/\left(q-2\right)
, then the modified gravity part, i.e.,
the second term of the l.h.s. of Eq. (
445
)
evolves as
\left(t_{s}-t\right)^{\left(u-1\right)\left(q-1\right)-2}
.
The consistency thus gives
\alpha=q
.
Additionally, if we consider that the leading term of the
non-minimal gravitational electromagnetic coupling sector should
not diverge in the limit
t\to t_{s}
, then
\alpha
must satisfy
the relation
\alpha\geq\left(u+1\right)/\left(u-1\right)
.
Taking only the leading terms in Eq. (
445
) and
using
\alpha=q
, one gets
Bamba:2008ut

\displaystyle I_{s}=\frac{a_{s}^{4}F_{1}}{|\bar{B}|^{2}\kappa^{2}}\,.

(449)

When
q>1
and
u\geq q/\left(q-2\right)
, then the leading term of
the modified-gravity sector evolves as
